Pochettino, Howe, Emery... or Carrick? Man Utd's managerial options

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

With one victory in their first 10 Premier League matches, Manchester United have made their worst start to a top-flight campaign since the 1986-87 season.

They are 18th in the table, one point above bottom club Crystal Palace.

United directors will meet in Monaco on Wednesday to discuss Michael Carrick's future as his 10-month spell as head coach comes under major scrutiny.

If the 45-year-old former player departs, it would mean an eighth permanent boss for the Red Devils since Sir Alex Ferguson retired 13 years ago.

That would be as many managerial changes as they have made across the previous 68 years dating back to Sir Matt Busby's appointment in 1945.

BBC Sport takes a look at the leading candidates who could replace Carrick.

Mauricio Pochettino penned a new deal with with the USA in the summer [Getty Images]

Major trophies won: 1 Ligue 1, 1 French Cup

Premier League record: P294, W150; Win percentage: 51; Points per game ratio (PPG): 1.77

Did you know: Pochettino averaged 1.89 points per game during his spell at Tottenham, the best ratio of any Spurs manager to have taken charge of 25 or more Premier League games.

United's long-standing interest in the Argentine, 54, dates back a decade.

At Spurs, he moulded their best team in a generation, but ultimately came up short of winning a trophy, losing both the Champions League and League Cup finals, and posted second and third-placed finishes. Silverware did come at Paris St-Germain with a league title, but a spell at Chelsea proved tougher with a League Cup final defeat by Liverpool in 2024.

Having led the USA to the last 16 of the World Cup on home soil he signed a new contract taking him up to the 2030 tournament. Pochettino has an exciting group of young players to work with for the national team - led by Manchester City-bound 17-year-old Cavan Sullivan - but he has always made it clear he has unfinished business in the Premier League.

A reported buyout clause in Pochettino's contract is worth $10million (£7.44m), while it is also understood to contain clauses that would allow him to leave for specific high-profile jobs.

Carrick's Man Utd future to be decided at Wednesday meeting

What Howe could bring to Man Utd - and what are the challenges?

Julian Nagelsmann resigned in the summer after Germany lost to Paraguay on penalties in the World Cup [Getty Images]

Did you know: Only Hansi Flick (88.9%) has a better win percentage by a Bayern Munich manager in the Champions League than Nagelsmann's 83.3%.

Nagelsmann first impressed with his work at Hoffenheim, where he became the youngest head coach in Bundesliga history aged 28, leading them from relegation struggles to the Champions League.

He followed that by taking RB Leipzig to a Champions League semi-final in 2020 and Bayern Munich understandably came calling.

But despite winning the Bundesliga title in his first season he reportedly fell out with key players and did not survive a full second season.

In 2023, Nagelsmann took charge of a Germany side in transition, and they were narrowly beaten in the quarter-finals of Euro 2024 by eventual winners Spain, but stepped down in the summer after Die Mannschaft were humbled by Paraguay in the World Cup last 32.

Still only 39, his reputation may have taken a bit of a hit, but the German is still regarded as one of Europe's best coaches. Manchester United's director of recruitment Christopher Vivell also knows him well, having worked together at RB Leipzig.

Eddie Howe oversaw more Premier League wins (84) than any other Newcastle manager [BBC Sport]

Premier League record: P369 W140; Win percentage: 37.9; PPG: 1.36

Did you know: Eddie Howe (five) is one of only three managers with five or more wins against Mikel Arteta in all competitions, along with Pep Guardiola (11) and Jurgen Klopp (six).

His body of work in English football needs little introduction having led Bournemouth to the top flight for the first time in their history, before he confirmed his place in Newcastle folklore with their first major domestic trophy since lifting the FA Cup in 1955.

The League Cup final triumph over Liverpool was one of the greatest days in the club's history, while he also secured two top-five finishes to take the club back into the Champions League for the first time in 20 years.

Howe, 48, left in the summer after an underwhelming 12th-placed finish last term and Newcastle would only be due a a relatively small amount of compensation should he be tempted by the Old Trafford job.

Antonio Conte called Tottenham's players "selfish" when he left after 16 months in 2023 [Getty Images]

Major trophies won: 5 Serie A, 1 Premier League, 1 FA Cup

Premier League record: P132, W83; Win percentage: 62.9; PPG: 2.03

Did you know: Chelsea won 30 of their 38 Premier League matches as they claimed the title in Antonio Conte's first season in charge in 2016-17.

A serial winner, Conte's managerial credentials are not in doubt.

He left Napoli of his own accord this summer just 12 months after delivering only the fourth top-flight title in their history.

That added to Serie A triumphs at Juventus (three) and Inter Milan, and spells at Chelsea and Tottenham.

Conte, 57, would appear to be a no-brainer of a choice to take over at United, but could the Italian's fiery nature and history of falling out with clubs count against him?

His last stint in English football ended under a cloud of controversy as he left Tottenham in 2023 following criticism of the players and the club's culture.

While he had success at Chelsea, Conte also reportedly clashed with both players and the board.

Emery has also managed Arsenal and Paris St-Germain in his career [Getty Images]

Major trophies won: 5 Europa League, 1 Ligue 1, 2 French Cups, 2 French League Cups

Premier League record: P200, W101; Win percentage: 50.5; PPG: 1.75

Did you know: Unai Emery has the best Premier League win percentage (51%) and points per game ratio (1.75) of any permanent Aston Villa boss.

Vastly experienced, the Spaniard's stock is at an all-time high courtesy of the superb job he has done since taking over at Villa in October 2022.

Having taken the club into the European Cup/Champions League for the first time for 41 years, the 55-year-old then ended their 30-year wait for silverware with success in last season's Europa League.

Despite having lost the spine of his team over the summer, Emery's new-look side are finding their feet again and the chances of him leaving for United look even slimmer with recent news he is expected to sign a new contract.

Michael Carrick's Manchester United side have managed one win in 10 league games this season [Getty Images]

Premier League record: P29, W14; Win percentage: 48.3; PPG: 1.76

Did you know: After his initial appointment as United's interim boss in January, Michael Carrick had the most wins (12) and points (39) of any Premier League manager during the rest of the 2025-26 season.

Still clinging on to his job in the Old Trafford hot seat, could United's hierarchy actually decide to keep faith with Carrick?

After such a brilliant start and end to the season following his arrival in January, the wheels have come off spectacularly this term with only three wins from 15 matches in all competitions.

His team have at least shown fight with comebacks in recent games to beat Roma and salvage a point in a 4-4 draw with Aston Villa, but their overall stats are damning and the outlook doesn't look good for the 45-year-old, who won five Premier League titles and the Champions League as a United player.

The club are in the relegation zone and 18th is their lowest position after 10 games of a season for 40 years. It is also only the second time they have gone eight Premier League games without a victory.

Airline Interline, Codeshare, Alliance, And Joint Venture Agreements Explained

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Airline partnerships are more complex and advanced than ever before, both for better and worse. About 25 years ago the Star Alliance was formed, which was revolutionary at the time. SkyTeam and oneworld quickly joined the scene.

While they all still exist, the global alliances have definitely been deemphasized a bit in recent years. After all, with these alliances having so many members, how much can loosely affiliated airlines really have in common?

Airlines want to work with other airlines that complement them best, and this can come in many different forms. Just to give a few examples of partnerships outside of the traditional alliances:

Airlines have several different levels of cooperation, ranging from interline agreements, to codeshare agreements, to alliance membership, to joint venture agreements. In this post, I’d like to take a closer at the differences between these agreements. Before I do so, let me add two disclaimers:

With that out of the way, let’s take a look at these four kinds of agreements.

Interline agreements are the most basic kinds of agreements you can have between airlines. An interline agreement is simply a commercial agreement between airlines to handle passengers when they’re traveling on multiple airlines on the same itinerary, and it allows an airline to issue a ticket on another airline (though not necessarily on any sort of favorable commercial terms).

This allows passengers to check their bags through to their final destination, get boarding passes all way to their destination, potentially be rebooked on another airline in the event of irregular operations, etc.

This is a basic level of cooperation, so there are airlines that have interline agreements but don’t otherwise have robust partnerships. For example, Etihad doesn’t belong to a major global alliance, but has an interline agreement with dozens of airlines, ranging from American, to Emirates, to JetBlue, to United, to Qatar Airways.

As you can see, this is a level of cooperation we even see between airlines that don’t partner with one another, and that may not even be fond of one another.

It is worth noting that in some cases airlines specifically pull out of interline agreements. For example, many years ago Delta cut its interline agreement with American. Why? At least in part it’s because Delta found that American was rebooking more passengers on Delta during irregular operations than the other way around, so the carrier no longer thought the arrangement was in its best interest.

A codeshare agreement is the next level of cooperation between airlines. This is when two airlines realize there’s value in working together, and they decide they want to place their “codes” on one another’s flights. Typically the main benefit of this is that it drums up business for the airlines that are in a codeshare agreement with one another.

Just to give an example, Alaska Airlines and Icelandair have a codeshare agreement. Icelandair flies to certain gateways in the United States, and then Alaska Airlines provides some of the “feed” for those flights in the form of codeshares.

For example, if you want to fly from Reykjavik to Los Angeles, Icelandair doesn’t operate that route. However, Icelandair flies to Portland, and then you can book a Portland to Los Angeles flight on Alaska Airlines as an Icelandair codeshare.

When you book a codeshare flight, you’ll notice that the flight number is different. So if you booked through Icelandair, your Portland to Los Angeles flight would also have an Icelandair flight number (in this case FI7696). To be clear:

With these codeshare agreements, generally neither airline is getting a huge cut for booking someone on the other airline. Most of the ticket revenue goes to the operating carrier, and often a small amount goes to the airline that sold the flight. However, the biggest advantage of this arrangement is to boost business for both airlines by increasing the number of flights that passengers have access to.

The “big three” alliances — oneworld, SkyTeam, and Star Alliance — have only developed in the past 25 years, though in the meantime many major global carriers belong to one of them. The basic way that alliances work is as follows:

I’d note that different airlines place different levels of emphasis on these alliances, even if they choose to belong to them. For example, Delta is a founding member of SkyTeam, but the airline is heavily focused on joint ventures and airlines it owns a stake in, and cares very little about the airlines it simply shares an alliance with.

If a codeshare agreement is like dating, then a joint venture is like getting married. A joint venture agreement is a massive business decision that typically requires extensive government approval. When airlines form a joint venture they coordinate pricing and schedules, and have a revenue sharing agreement.

Exactly how the revenue sharing works depends on the specific arrangement, but the idea is that two airlines are essentially acting as one under a joint venture.

Note that typically airlines form joint ventures between specific regions, which is why this is different than an outright merger. For example, American has a transatlantic joint venture with British Airways, Finnair, Iberia, and Aer Lingus, while the airline has two transpacific joint ventures, with both Japan Airlines and Qantas (the two are separate arrangements).

From the perspective of a consumer, a joint venture is both good and bad. The good news is that typically it gives you the most flight options in terms of schedules, since the airlines are operating as one. Airlines also often try to make the experience as consistent across brands as possible. The downside is that it’s like a competitor being eliminated in the market, so it could lead to higher fares as it reduces competition.

There are so many different types of agreements in the airline industry nowadays. While the exact terms vary with every partnership, I think the easiest way to sum it up is that an interline agreement is like a friendship, a codeshare agreement is like an engagement, a joint venture is like a marriage, and an alliance is like having a big family, with everyone sort of doing their own thing.

There’s a lot more to all of this, but hopefully the above provides a pretty fair and basic summary. People often misuse these terms, so I figure it’s worth recapping every so often.

¡Vamos, España!

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

With Spain fresh off a World Cup win, there’s no better time to visit Madrid and the cooler northern regions of Asturias and Galicia

I lived in Spain in the early aughts, when the country was experiencing a mic-drop moment. The shiny, futuristic Frank Gehry–designed Guggenheim Museum had gone up in Bilbao a few years earlier, setting off what is now known as the Bilbao effect: a phenomenon that describes how investing in a visionary architecture project can transform a place. Food-obsessed travelers were flocking to a small beach town in a remote corner of northern Spain just to experience a meal at El Bulli, the legendary, now closed, three-Michelin-star restaurant headed up by Ferran Adrià.

Spain is now in the midst of another glow-up, thanks in part to its World Cup win, and Madrid is front and center. A recent influx of expats from South and North America has locals calling the city the “Miami of Europe.” But don’t let that dissuade you. Mallorquín designer Rosa Esteva, who designs the elegant, made-in-Spain fashion brand Cortana, emphasizes “the charm of those glorious old places that have survived through time.” That means staying at hotels like the intimate Hotel Orfila, booking a table at La Parra, wandering through El Rastro, Madrid’s legendary flea market, and checking out a late-night flamenco show at Tablao de la Villa. At the same time, contemporary places like Esteva’s new shop in the Salamanca neighborhood, the modern tapas bar Sala de Despiece, and the contemporary-art gallery Albarrán Bourdais are worth checking out.

The typical Spanish itinerary often includes a few days in Barcelona to take in the Gaudí before heading south to the beaches of Andalusia. But Vella González, the founder of fashion brand Boxcalf, which has a flagship in Madrid’s Malasaña neighborhood, extols northern Spain’s charms and cooler climate. “For non-Spaniards, it’s an undiscovered paradise,” she says. Start in San Sebastián, one of the world’s most celebrated gastronomic capitals, and eat your way through its pintxos bars, finishing with a steak at the Basque grill house Portuetxe.

In Bilbao, discover the next generation of art spaces, such as the light-filled expansion of the Bilbao Fine Arts Museum, designed by Norman Foster and Luis María Uriarte. The Bilbao effect can be felt in nearby Santander, thanks to the new Faro Santander museum and cultural space, a historic building reimagined by David Chipperfield and currently showcasing the works of surrealist painter Leonora Carrington. Move on to the pristine beaches, green hills, and exceptional dining scene of the Asturias and Galicia regions. In Asturias, splurge on a stay at the remote wabi-sabi styled Solo Palacio and explore the old-growth forests and prehistoric rock shelters of Las Ubiñas-La Mesa Natural Park. Cozy up in the Casa Beatnik Country House hotel in Galicia, and walk the last 10 miles of the famed Camino de Santiago trail to Santiago de Compostela and its Old Town, a UNESCO World Heritage site. It’s an idyllic ending to a new kind of Spanish pilgrimage.

XRP Update: Did Former Ripple CTO David Schwartz Sell 2.5 Million XRP?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

David Schwartz, Ripple’s former chief technology officer and one of the original architects of the XRP Ledger, has said he sold about 2.5 million XRP in 2025. He disclosed the sale on X while responding to a critic and said he paid about $1.7 million in capital gains tax on it.

The disclosure came during one of XRP’s worst weeks in months, as frustration over the token’s price grew across the community.

A user replying to Schwartz called XRP a terrible investment and said nobody would blame him for selling. Schwartz answered that the $1.7 million he paid in capital gains tax “says otherwise.”

He said most of the XRP he sold in 2025 came from bonuses Ripple paid him for his work in 2020 and 2023. His point was that large capital gains tax bills come from profitable investments, not losing ones.

What The Tax Bill Suggests About His Sale Price

Schwartz did not say what price he sold at. A breakdown by one crypto analyst used his tax figure to estimate it:

That works out to about $2 to $2.40 per XRP if California’s top rate applied, and closer to $3 if no state tax was paid. Because his exact cost basis and tax situation are not public, the estimate covers a wider range of $2 to $3.50. XRP reached $3.65 in July 2025, its highest level in seven years.

There is no sign the sale was based on inside information. Schwartz has said in the past that, around 2012, he and his wife agreed to sell some of their crypto every time it hit a new high, to lock in gains and cut risk.

“I really, really hate risk,” Schwartz has said.

According to his past comments, that rule led him to sell Bitcoin at $750, sell 40,000 Ether at about $1 each and begin selling XRP at around $0.10. He has said he now regrets some of those sales.

Schwartz has said he once held about 26 million XRP and now holds about 2 million. He has described Ripple’s stock, not XRP, as his main bet in the industry.

He has also said he does not want XRP’s price to depend on how Ripple performs as a company. Schwartz originally sold Bitcoin to buy XRP, and has said XRP has made him more money than either Bitcoin or Ethereum.

The disclosure lands at a sensitive time. XRP is trading below $1.10, about 70% under its record high, and many holders are questioning Ripple’s commitment to the token. Some critics may read the sale as an insider cashing out.

Brandon Aiyuk likely will be cut by 4:00 p.m. ET today

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A key fact has come into focus regarding the Brandon Aiyuk situation. And it points to a likely conclusion to the 49ers’ portion of the situation by 4:00 p.m. ET today.

Although it’s not clear how this happened (the Player Personnel Manual indicates that his 2025 salary of $1.17 million should have tolled into 2026 due to placement on the reserve/left squad list), official NFL records now show Aiyuk as having a base salary of $13.75 million for the balance of 2026. This means that, if he’s on the roster at the close of business on Tuesday, his salary for Week 10 vests — at $1.52 million.

They surely won’t be able to trade him at that number. And they surely don’t want to give him $1.52 million. Even more surely, coach Kyle Shanahan doesn’t want him in the building.

There’s still a story to be told as to the deal that was done to get the league to reinstate Aiyuk. The NFL and 49ers were putting the squeeze on Aiyuk to refund upwards of $13 million in previously-paid bonus money.

Our guess? Aiyuk wired a sizable payment to the 49ers (maybe $6 million or $7 million), the NFL reinstated him, and now Aiyuk will get his freedom.

The next question will become where he lands.

14 traits show where Congress matches the U.S. public and where it falls short

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

A look at 14 traits of the 119th Congress, from age and prior jobs to religion and military service, and how they compare with the public.

Each new Congress tends to arrive with a headline about the record it just broke, and that habit makes it easy to miss how far the body still trails the people it governs. The gains themselves have been steady. For the eighth time running, a newly seated Congress has set a mark for racial and ethnic diversity, according to Pew Research Center. Even after that streak, the people who write federal law remain overwhelmingly white, with non-White lawmakers making up 26% of the 119th Congress. The country has changed faster than its legislature, and racial and ethnic minorities now account for 42% of the U.S. population. Breaking records and closing a gap are different achievements. On race, Congress has done the first many times over without yet doing the second.

The newer faces in Congress also come largely through one party. Pew Research Center reports that 84% of the Black, Hispanic, Asian American and Native American voting members of Congress are Democrats. Women are spread just as unevenly, filling 42% of the Democratic seats in Congress and 15% of the Republican ones, according to Pew Research Center. Together those figures place both kinds of diversity mostly on one side of the aisle. How closely Congress resembles the country therefore rises and falls with which party wins seats, and it doesn't depend only on slow, long-term change in who Americans are. That holds for more than race and gender, and the party behind each trait is worth checking.

Congressional Research Service set out to describe the demographic and career traits of lawmakers in the current Congress, from members' ages and the jobs they held before office to their religions and military service. Members' schooling, careers and earlier elected office are part of that picture too. Looking at those stages of a lawmaker's life changes the question from who wins seats to what kind of person usually becomes a lawmaker. Taken trait by trait, Congress is far from the public on some measures and has caught up on others, while on still others change is only starting at its edges.

Congress adds new faces mostly by replacing old ones. The current Congress has 541 members, according to the Congressional Research Service. Of those, 435 represent House districts, one member for each district, according to the Census Bureau. A newcomer can win one of those district seats only after the member who held it loses, retires or otherwise leaves. Each new diversity record is therefore a story about who left as well as who arrived, and each gain depends on an opening.

Very few Americans have ever held a seat in Congress. Since 1789, only 12,585 people have served, across more than two centuries of the nation's history. The lawmakers serving today are only the most recent members of that long, narrow line of officeholders. The profile on the slides that follow describes who holds a scarce set of seats at this moment. It does not describe a body that keeps opening its doors wider.

Who wins House seats depends on where district lines fall, and those lines stay in place across several elections. The districts used from the 118th through the 122nd Congress are based on 2020 Census results, according to the Census Bureau. House candidates therefore compete on the same maps for years at a time. A comparison between this Congress and those of earlier decades sets different maps against each other as well as different people.

Each trait ahead shows who won a limited number of seats on a given set of maps, not proof that Congress has grown more open.

Republicans run the House, but their majority is about as narrow as a working majority can be. The party holds 219 House seats to the Democrats' 212, according to the Congressional Research Service. NIH reports that control of a full House takes 218 of its 435 seats, so Republicans have very few seats to spare. Republican leaders need nearly every one of their members present and voting with them to pass a bill without Democratic help. A few absences or defections can defeat a measure that a larger majority would pass with ease. The membership of the House also changes during a session. Four House seats were vacant when the count was taken, which lowers the number of votes a majority needs while those seats stay empty. The party totals therefore describe one moment rather than a fixed balance, and the Republican margin stays thin either way.

Republicans won the Senate outright in 2024 instead of only narrowing a Democratic lead. Democrats controlled the chamber going into that election, NIH reports, and Republicans now hold 53 of its seats, according to the Congressional Research Service. Voters moved the Senate from one party to the other rather than leaving the same party in charge with a smaller margin. Together, the two chambers show how unevenly Republicans gained control of Congress. In the Senate they took power away from Democrats. In the House they kept power with only a narrow cushion.

The Senate shows how quickly voters can give a chamber to the other party, and the House shows how few seats would have to change for them to do it again.

Voters send new members to Washington every election, yet the House has not grown any younger. The average representative was 57.9 years old when the 119th Congress opened, exactly the same average as at the start of the 118th, according to the Congressional Research Service. A full round of departures and arrivals ended with the chamber at the same age. The Senate is older still, with an average senator age of 63.9. Most senators belong to the baby boom generation, Pew Research Center reports, and that majority helps account for how much older the Senate is than the House. In the Senate, a single generation still holds most of the seats.

Congress gets its younger members through newcomers, and this year's House newcomers were younger than the chamber they joined but older than the class before them. Newly elected representatives averaged 50.2 years, well below the House average, while the newcomers of the 118th Congress had averaged 47.8. Even with a freshman class well below the average, the House as a whole ended up no younger than before. This class also narrowed the age difference between newcomers and the chamber they joined.

In the House, age does not divide along party lines. Pew Research Center reports that House Democrats and House Republicans have nearly the same median age, so a younger House would have to come from members of both sides. The newest generation barely has a foothold. Rep. Maxwell Frost, a Florida Democrat, is the youngest member of the House and its only member of Generation Z.

One class of younger newcomers was not enough to lower the House average, and a younger Congress will take more than that.

Before they came to Congress, most members worked in politics itself, not in any one private profession. Some 305 representatives, making up 70% of the House, had served in an elected post somewhere in government, from city hall to the statehouse to Washington, according to the Congressional Research Service. A representative who had never run for office and won is the exception. Senators are even more likely to have held office before, and 82 of them, or 82%, had done so. Members bring many careers to Capitol Hill, but nearly all of them first learned to campaign and govern at a lower level.

Law is the profession most closely tied to Congress, and lawyers are much more common in the Senate than in the House. Most House members never practiced law. In the Senate, however, 47% of members hold law degrees and practiced law. Senators are more likely than representatives to have held earlier office and more likely to have worked as lawyers. Voters in Senate races therefore pick people with both credentials more often than voters in House races do. Even in the Senate, though, a career in law is a common route rather than the usual one. Earlier elected office is the experience that most members share.

Members who came from the private sector were far more likely to have run a company than to have represented workers. Before taking office, at least 135 representatives, or 31% of the House, had started, owned or managed a business. That group is as large as the group of House members who practiced law. Only a handful of members ever worked as union representatives, and all of them serve in the House. No senator has that background. Members with experience running a business hold far more seats than members who once spoke for workers, and both groups vote on wages and workplace rules.

On education, Congress comes almost entirely from the part of the country that finished college. Among House members, 96% hold a bachelor's degree or higher, according to the Congressional Research Service. Most American adults lack such a degree, with the share of those 25 and older who held a bachelor's or more at 38% in 2021, according to Census Bureau figures reported by Pew Research Center. No law requires a diploma to run for Congress, yet the people voters send to the House almost always have one. The college degree marks only where the gap with the public starts.

For most House members, college was not the end of their schooling. Some 66% of representatives hold an advanced degree beyond the bachelor's, which makes graduate school the usual path into the House. The typical representative has studied a full level beyond the college degree that most of their constituents never earned.

The House has grown more credentialed over the decades, not less. In the 89th Congress, which met in 1965-1966, 74% of House members had a bachelor's degree. Since then, House members have become nearly all college graduates. On this trait, the House has moved further from the public instead of closer to it.

Congress has become more racially diverse over the decades, but on schooling its members have grown more alike.

A sizable share of the House was new to the job when this Congress began. Some 135 representatives, or 30.6% of the chamber, had served two years or less, according to the Congressional Research Service. That large minority of the House came into this session with little time in Congress behind them.

Experience runs deeper in the Senate, where 45 senators served in the House first. That is close to half the chamber. For those senators, the move across the Capitol came after years of learning how Congress works. Members who start in the House, build experience there and carry it to the Senate keep a core of career politicians in place.

This Congress is about to lose a large share of its members, including some of the most familiar names in both chambers. TIME reports that at least 70 House members will not return, among them Pelosi, Hoyer and Nadler, and that McConnell and Durbin are retiring from the Senate. First-term lawmakers will fill the open House seats. Pelosi, Hoyer, Nadler, McConnell and Durbin held seats at the top of Congress, and they are leaving together in a single cycle rather than one at a time.

Not every departure is voluntary, and the forced ones show that long service does not guarantee a seat. Twelve incumbents in the House and Senate who sought reelection lost renomination, according to TIME. These members did not choose to step aside. Their own party picked someone else as its nominee. Time in office did not protect them in either chamber.

The steady, experienced core of senators who came up through the House belongs to this Congress alone, and the next one will likely be less experienced at the top as well as at the edges.

On religion, the Americans Congress most clearly leaves out are defined by belief: people who claim no religion. Almost every lawmaker names a faith, with 96% reporting a specific affiliation, according to the Congressional Research Service. Yet 28% of American adults claim no religious affiliation at all, according to Pew Research Center figures reported by EWTN News. Americans without a religion are a large minority of the adult public, but only a small share of lawmakers either decline to name a faith or report having none.

Most of the seats such Americans might hold go instead to members of one tradition. Protestants fill 55.1% of the seats in Congress but make up 40% of the public, according to Pew Research Center figures reported by EWTN News, so Congress has a Protestant majority that the country itself does not have. On belief, Congress is more religious and more Protestant than the people who elect it.

Lawmakers from Congress's minority faiths come almost entirely from one party. EWTN News reports that the Democratic caucus includes all of the lawmakers who identify as Muslim, Hindu, Buddhist, Unitarian Universalist or humanist. The Muslim and Hindu members form small groups, and all of them serve in the House. Republicans hold a small faith bloc of their own, because all nine Latter-day Saints in Congress are Republicans. Each of these groups of lawmakers comes from a single party, so its numbers can rise or fall with one election. Congress's religious variety therefore depends on which side wins seats more than on the beliefs of the country.

Women outnumber men among American adults, yet they hold well under a third of the seats in Congress. Pew Research Center reports that women make up 51% of Americans ages 25 and older, but they hold just 28.65% of the seats in Congress, according to the Congressional Research Service. That leaves a majority of the adult public with well short of half the seats where federal law is written.

The number of women in Congress stopped rising with the last election. Congress has 155 women today, the same number that served when the 118th Congress ended, so a full election cycle produced no net gain. With the count flat, women's share is not moving toward half of Congress, and it will stay at its current level until the count grows.

Voters mostly returned women to office. In the House, 104 of 110 women incumbents won reelection in 2024, Pew Research Center reports, and the number of women in Congress still did not change. Women did win seats for the first time, but their arrivals only balanced out the women who left Congress. Future gains therefore depend on women winning more seats than women give up, whether those seats open through retirements, runs for other offices or defeats.

Until women gain more seats than they lose, the gap between Congress and the women who make up most of the adult public will stay where it is.

On Black representation, Congress has nearly caught up with the country it governs. Black lawmakers hold 12.2% of the seats in Congress, according to the Congressional Research Service, close to the share of Black Americans in the nation as a whole. Jenn Hatfield of Pew Research Center reports that Black Americans make up 14% of the U.S. population and that Black lawmakers' share of Congress is on par with it. That is a real milestone, but it belongs to one group. Parity for Black Americans does not mean Congress has caught up with minority Americans as a whole.

Most Black lawmakers serve in the House. Just five Black lawmakers serve in the Senate, and every other Black member of Congress holds a House seat. The parity Black Americans have reached applies to Congress as a whole, and it rests mainly on members of the larger chamber. Black voters see far more people who share their background in one half of the Capitol than in the other.

Nearly all Black lawmakers are also Democrats. Republicans count four Black members in the House and one Black senator. Black parity in Congress therefore rests mostly on members of the Democratic caucus, and any shift in that caucus's size can move the number of Black lawmakers along with it.

The close match between Congress and Black America holds only as long as the party that sends most Black lawmakers to Washington keeps its seats.

Hispanic Americans hold far fewer seats in Congress than their numbers in the country would suggest, even in a session that set a record for diversity. Hispanic lawmakers hold 10.35% of the seats in Congress, according to the Congressional Research Service. Pew Research Center reports that Hispanics make up 20% of the U.S. population. The records Congress has set for racial diversity in recent sessions have not reached Hispanic Americans in proportion to their numbers. As a result, Hispanic Americans hold a share of Congress well short of their share of the public.

That shortfall does not mean nothing has changed, because Hispanic representation has grown a great deal from a very small start. In the 99th Congress, which sat in 1985 and 1986, there were 14 Hispanic members, all of them men serving in the House. At that point the community had no senator and no woman in either chamber. The growth since then is real progress. But it began so far behind that today's much larger group of Hispanic lawmakers still holds a share of Congress well below the community's share of the population.

Hispanic lawmakers are not drawn almost entirely from one party. Twelve House Republicans are Hispanic, which means that the number of Hispanic members does not rest on Democratic wins alone. Most Hispanic lawmakers are still Democrats, so the lean toward one side remains. Even so, Hispanic candidates win seats as Republicans as well as Democrats, so either party's victories can add Hispanic members to Congress.

Closing the gap with the population does not have to wait on one party's fortunes, because both parties send Hispanic lawmakers to Congress.

Asian and Pacific Islander lawmakers show a Congress that is still early in the process of changing. The group holds 24 seats, or 4.4% of Congress, according to the Congressional Research Service, which makes it a small presence among the people who write federal law. Even that small group is a large gain over the 99th Congress of 1985-1986, when Asian and Pacific Islander lawmakers held five House seats and two Senate seats. Its current size shows how far the group has come from a very small base, and it should not be read as a settled level.

Democrats hold most of those seats, which ties the group's presence in Congress closely to one party. Democrats account for 16 of the group's House members and Republicans for five, while all three senators in the group are Democrats. Asian and Pacific Islander representation in Congress therefore rests largely on the members of a single party.

Women in this group took their seats only after the mid-1980s. Every Asian and Pacific Islander member of the 99th Congress was a man, so each woman in the group today joined a delegation that had no women four decades ago. Asian and Pacific Islander women in Congress are therefore a recent development, with no earlier generation of women in the group before them.

The group's gains are real, and all of them have come within the past four decades.

Native Americans hold fewer seats than any other group in this profile of Congress, so few that a single election can change the picture. Congress had four Native American members as of August 2025, according to the Congressional Research Service. With a group that small, one arrival or one departure changes the total by a large share, so the count describes a moment rather than a settled level. In the Senate, where Native members have been rarest, the count is already out of date.

The Senate has been the hardest chamber for Native politicians to reach, and its record is only now starting to change. In the more than 230 years since the chamber was established, just four Native senators have held seats there, every one of them a man, according to The Associated Press. That record ends with Peggy Flanagan, who will be the first Native woman to serve in the Senate when she takes office.

Native lawmakers do not follow the pattern in which most of Congress's new diversity arrives through Democrats. Two of the three Native House members are Republicans and one is a Democrat, and the one sitting Native senator is also a Republican. Flanagan is a Democrat, according to The Associated Press. Once she is seated, the Senate will have Native members from both parties. For this group, a lawmaker's party does not predict whether that lawmaker adds to the diversity of Congress, and each group has to be judged on its own terms.

Native candidates are winning state offices as well as seats in Congress. New Mexico voters made Deb Haaland the first Native woman to win a governor's race in any state, according to The Associated Press. Her victory adds to the number of Native leaders who have held high office. "This is a seismic shift that is happening in our communities," said Jordan James Harvill, a Cherokee Nation citizen and strategist with Advance Native Political Leadership. Native members still hold 0.74% of the seats in Congress, so most of that shift has not yet reached the Capitol.

Congress is where this rise will show up last, and the low count reflects a group that is arriving late, not one that has reached its limit.

Congress is far less foreign-born than the country it governs. About 6.0% of members of the 119th Congress were born abroad, according to the Congressional Research Service. Across the country the share is much higher: In 2024, 15.4% of the people living in the U.S. had come from another country, according to Pew Research Center. Some members born abroad were born to American citizens, so even fewer lawmakers are immigrants than the foreign-born count suggests. Americans who moved here from another country have far fewer lawmakers who share that background than their numbers in the population would suggest.

Birthplace alone understates how close Congress is to the immigrant experience. Counting lawmakers whose parents came from abroad along with those who came themselves, at least 15% of Congress has immigrant roots, according to Pew Research Center. That group is far larger than the members born abroad. Many of these lawmakers are therefore the children of immigrants rather than immigrants themselves, born in the U.S. to parents who were not. When Congress takes up immigration policy, their family histories bring that experience into the debate as well.

Lawmakers with immigrant roots also sit mostly on one side of the aisle. Most of them are Democrats, and far fewer are Republicans, according to Pew Research Center. The number of lawmakers who know immigration from their own families therefore depends heavily on how many seats Democrats hold after each election.

The part of Congress that carries the immigrant story includes many children of immigrants and sits mostly in one party, and a single election can shrink it.

Most members of Congress have never served in the military. The current Congress has 98 veterans, or 18.1% of its members, according to the Congressional Research Service. That leaves most of the lawmakers who vote on war, the Pentagon budget and veterans' care without that experience of their own. The number has stopped falling, though. The 118th Congress also opened with 98 veterans, so the count was the same at the start of two Congresses in a row. On this trait, Congress is not setting new records. The question is whether its veterans can keep the number steady.

Veterans once made up a far larger share of Congress. In the 92nd Congress, which met in 1971 and 1972 during the Vietnam War, 73% of lawmakers were veterans. Many current members were alive to see a Congress in which military service was the norm. Since then, it has gone from a body in which most members knew military life firsthand to one in which most rely on the accounts of others. Lawmakers now decide whether to send troops into combat, and how to care for them when they come home, mostly without having served themselves.

Women make up a small part of the veterans in Congress. Nine women in Congress are veterans, and three of them saw combat. They are a small group among the 98 veterans, but their presence shows that the people who bring military experience to Congress do not all come from a single background.

Military service has become less common in Congress, and the members who still have it include women who served in combat.

Messina Touring Group Looks To The Future: ‘As Things Are Changing, We Adapt’

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The company's president of Nashville Haley McCollister reflects on Louis Messina's mentorship and belief in his staff.

The only job Haley McCollister has ever had has been at Messina Touring Group. Since joining the company’s ticketing department at 22 years old, she’s worked her way up and now serves as president of MTG’s Nashville office.

“The more he believed in me, the more I believed in myself,” McCollister says.

McCollister notes that many of her MTG colleagues have been with the company since they started their first jobs out of college. This is due to company founder Louis Messina’s strong belief in each employee, with McCollister likening his support to how he strengthens his relationships with his artists.

Aside from McCollister, key members of the next generation at MTG include senior vp Sara Winter, vp of touring Keena Cheatham and vp Rachel Powers.

As the company continues to support its promoters, Messina has been passing more responsibilities onto his fellow teammates and providing them opportunities to foster their own artists. MTG maintains a small artist roster, wanting to provide all its attention to each client rather than having each person focus on multiple acts.

“We’re not out there chasing everything,” McCollister says. “To keep it this way, we have to grow very slowly. To find the ‘right’ artist for us to grow with starts with someone being extremely passionate about them and feeling it’s the right fit. And Louis is really supportive of us.”

McCollister and her colleagues fell in love with Phoebe Bridgers while she was opening for Taylor Swift on The Eras Tour and began discussions about bringing her into the fold. Messina came on board, and Bridgers is now promoted by MTG.

As the company looks ahead to its next quarter-­century, the team members feel they’re operating in a way they hope to be able to sustain.

“For the next 25 years, it’s going to look similar to what we’ve already done,” McCollister says. “As the business continues to change, that doesn’t impact how we operate, because we’ve always operated unique to each individual artist. As things are changing, we adapt with them and are continuing to deliver long-term success.”

This story appears in the Nov. 7, 2026, issue of Billboard.

Yellowstone's Downfall Can Be Traced Back To One Moment

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There was a time when "Yellowstone" was on top of the world, so far as mainstream television was concerned. What started as a somewhat unassuming Western TV show on an upper-tier cable channel became a breakout hit, in no small part thanks to the participation of Oscar-winner Kevin Costner in the lead role as John Dutton. "Yellowstone" set a huge record as the most-watched show of 2022. It was massive. Then, it all came crashing down.

"Yellowstone" Season 5 became its last, with a massive gap between "Part 1" and "Part 2" of that season. Series creator Taylor Sheridan decided to focus more on the show's spin-offs, as well as his other projects, with the series finale airing in December 2024. That was that. But where, exactly, did it all go wrong? When was the downfall of this once mighty TV titan? Frankly, it's not that hard to figure out.

To put my cards on the table, I've not seen a single episode of the show. I'm merely someone who writes about this stuff professionally and pays attention to the business. Anyone who does that can tell you with confidence that the behind-the-scenes drama between Sheridan and Costner is without a doubt what doomed "Yellowstone." Once Costner checked out, however that actually went down, the writing was on the wall.

Indeed, it was reported in May 2023 that Costner was leaving "Yellowstone" after Season 5. That raised eyebrows and sounded alarms. Any time a lead actor leaves a show, that's to be expected. But as the headlines continued to swirl, it became clear that this wasn't just a guy stepping away from a series. There was much more to the story.

To this day, it's still not entirely clear how everything went down. What we know for sure is that Taylor Sheridan became the busiest man in showbusiness, with a ton of series on the air, all of which he was chiefly responsible for. Kevin Costner, meanwhile, had other projects he wanted to do while also fronting "Yellowstone." Tension mounted. It even came to light that Costner was "very upset" with "Yellowstone" Season 2, long before his departure.

The gap between the end of "Part 1" of "Yellowstone" Season 5 and "Part 2" is where things really started to go south. Costner wanted to go make his long-planned Western movie saga "Horizon," which he did. But that conflicted with the planned schedule for the show, which caused issues. Not for nothing, we still don't know what's going on with "Horizon: An American Saga — Chapter 2" after the first movie bombed in theaters. But that's another story.

Sheridan has explained Costner's surprising "Yellowstone" exit, suggesting that the actor stayed on the show even longer than he wanted to in the first place:

"Even though Kevin was ready to go —he had other things he wanted to do — he stayed on for another two seasons, and that was just because the show was such a behemoth and such a huge hit that the notion of giving up a hit before it had run of juice to squeeze is very foreign to a network."

But it's not that simple. Costner explained his "Yellowstone" exit and said he'd "probably go to court" over it in 2023. It wasn't an amicable parting of ways. The main point is that Costner leaving and the feud that went on undoubtedly derailed the show. It's that simple.

It's also worth pointing out that even when Kevin Costner was still on the show, things were far from rosey. Costner's clash with a co-star reportedly left "Yellowstone" lead Kelly Reilly in tears, and while Costner has a bit of a reputation for being difficult to work with in general, reports like this are still a bad look. At a certain point, even wildly popular TV shows are forced to buckle to external pressures, particularly when they involve their leading men.

Be that as it may, Taylor Sheridan and Paramount have made the most out of the situation. It's a real lemons/lemonade deal they've got going on. Spoilers, but "Yellowstone" Season 5 dealt with John Dutton by revealing he's dead. The show moved on and got to its finale without him. More than that, Sheridan has made the most of the greater "Yellowstone" universe he established by not only developing prequels such as "1883" and "1923" but spin-offs as well.

First and foremost is "Dutton Ranch," which is getting a Season 2. What happens beyond that remains to be seen, but its protagonists Beth Dutton (Reilly) and Rip Wheeler (Cole Hauser) have effectively picked up where John Dutton left off. There's also "Marshals," which has brought Luke Grimes' Kayce Dutton to network TV, resulting in a big hit on CBS. So, even without Costner, the "Yellowstone" franchise has thrived. It wasn't a happy marriage or a happy divorce, but the dust settled, and everyone figured out a path forward.

Google Pixel Tag Initial Review: The gold standard for Find Hub trackers, whatever that’s worth

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Google’s Find Hub had a tumultuous launch for its performance, but also for a lack of clear direction in hardware. Over two years later, that’s finally arriving in the Google Pixel Tag which, even just a few days in, is the obvious gold standard in the race to build an AirTag for Android devices.

There are plenty of really good Android Find Hub trackers on the market. Pebblebee and Chipolo have been long-standing examples of how to build good dual-network trackers that work on both Android and iOS, while Motorola’s Moto Tag series has delivered two Android-only trackers that deliver better performance and more focused features. But even with the Moto Tag, there’s a clear compromise. Features like the ability to ring your phone are tied to a separate app and, in turn, have some inherent clunkiness.

Pixel Tag is the device that finally brings it all together, and it’s brilliant so far.

I’ve only had the Pixel Tag for a week and, frankly, it’s impossible to properly review a tracker in that time. There are only so many ways to artificially test out how a tracker works, as most cases where you’ll find value in this sort of product can only occur in real life.

So, with that said, let’s focus on the fundamentals.

Google Pixel Tag pairs to your Android phone just like any other Find Hub tracker – though much faster compared to some others I’ve tested – but the differences are obvious towards the end of that process. The final screen shows options for setting ringer volume and an option to set up “Ring this phone.”

Those new features are what make the Pixel Tag so good, and so appealing compared to all of the competition. A quick double-tap on the Tag’s button rings whichever phone you connected it to, assuming it’s in range, and it works pretty reliably. You can also set up “left behind” alerts (now available on other Find Hub trackers too) for when the phone leaves the tracker behind – you can set up exclusion zones such as your home or work address.

One of my favorite examples of the added polish from Google is the ability to adjust the ringer volume on the page where you actually want to ring the tracker. In a crowded but quiet space where you know the tracker is close by, being able to quickly set the volume to low is a great addition. In my book, it’s the reason to buy Pixel Tag over any other Android tracker.

UWB, of course, only makes up-close finding all the better. I found this decently reliable on my Pixel 11 Pro XL, but it is a little bit frustrating that the directional cues vanish within about 10-12 feet. Fine for a backpack or something like that, potentially a problem if your Tag is attached to a set of keys.

On that note, my biggest hardware complaint is that Google took the Apple route of having no built-in mounting mechanism. The super thin and light hardware of Pixel Tag is wonderful, don’t get me wrong, but being forced to buy a separate accessory just to attach it to my bag is frustrating! One of Google’s partners, Bellroy, built one of these accessories and it’s simple enough – a silicone case with a rope. I wish I didn’t need it, but it works, and at least you get two of them in a pack for $25.

The hardware itself is pretty good across the board, really, with the battery door also being slightly easier to open compared to the Moto Tag. Inside is a standard CR2032 battery that Google says will last “over one year.” I’d personally still prefer a rechargeable battery with USB-C or wireless charging, but this is a good enough solution given the amount of time the battery lasts – hopefully Google’s claim lives up.

Rounding out some of the key aspects here, it’s worth saying that the built-in speaker is more than loud enough at its highest setting, but the value for me so far has really come in that the low setting is actually low enough to not be a nuisance. I do find it a little strange that there’s no visual indication of where the Tag’s button is, but the packaging and app both do a good enough job of letting you know.

Overall performance in Find Hub seems fine, but as mentioned at the outset, this is a really hard thing to test artificially, all the more so in the span of just a few days. Google’s Find Hub network has gotten a lot better in the past two years, but it’s still hit or miss in my experience. It’s very clear that Google is making an effort to reverse the privacy-inspired decision to effectively disable the Find Hub network outside of crowded areas, but the damage has been done. I’m not sure the network will ever fully recover from that misstep, regardless of what tracker you’re using it with.

I’m looking forward to seeing how Pixel Tag performs as the travel season spins back up, so stay tuned for a later long-term review of Google’s tracker in the coming months. Initial impressions, though, are solid. Sound off in the comments below if you have any questions around Pixel Tag, I’ll answer everything I can!

If you’re interested in picking up a Pixel Tag, it’ll be available on November 11.

NFL Week 10 power rankings: Despite 4 straight wins, Detroit Lions sit behind NFC North rivals

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Detroit Lions (6-2) have now won four in a row, are 2-0 in the division with wins over the Packers and Vikings, and sit in second place in the NFC North, just a half-game back of the Chicago Bears. For the first time this season, the Lions rank in the top 10 of every national power ranking that we track.

But it's not all sunshine and roses in the Lions' relationship with the national media. Of the eight rankings we track, the Lions dropped this week in over half of them. Even more surprising, they dropped behind the Packers in two (Yahoo Sports and Sports Illustrated) and remain behind the Vikings in two others (Sporting News and ESPN)—despite having the same (Vikings) or better (Packers) record—and winning both head-to-head matchups.

Let's take a closer look at this week's national power rankings.

Having spent time in Buffalo after Damar Hamlin's near-death experience in a Bills-Bengals game, I can still only begin to fathom the heaviness this Lions team is dealing with on a daily basis in the aftermath of the Jack Campbell injury. It makes the Dolphins victory feel very different… and somehow more amazing.

WR Amon-Ra St. Brown hasn't scored since September and only has two 100-yard receiving games this season. Think the German-American star might break out Sunday in Munich − especially given how the Pats secondary just got shredded?

Sunday's victory wasn't pretty by any means, but sometimes a team needs to find a way to win an ugly one. The Lions did that in Miami, getting just enough from Jahmyr Gibbs to leave with a W. That's what I'd expect from a team in the top 10, but I'm hoping this was an informative experience to grow from, not a harbinger of things to come.

It hasn't always looked pretty (including in Miami on Sunday), and the underlying defensive stats (they rank 30th in EPA per play) remain concerning. But the offense has been among the game's best as they now head abroad to face the reeling New England defense.

It wasn't pretty against the Dolphins, but they found a way. They can't play that way this week against a wounded Patriots team or else.

Is the Lions' defense good? It has been better recently. For the third straight game the Lions' opponent failed to top 17 points. Two of those games happened against the offensively challenged Vikings and Dolphins; the third game in that streak was against the Packers. Advanced metrics are kind to the Lions' defense over the past three weeks too, as Detroit is eighth in EPA per play allowed and fifth in success rate allowed. Star safety Brian Branch returned from an Achilles injury three games ago, and it's not a coincidence the Lions' defense is much better with him back. The Lions have held opponents to 21 or fewer points in four straight games, the first time that has happened since 2016, according to the team's site.

The Lions allowed just 228 yards and 12 first downs Sunday, and while that was against Miami, it's still a lot better than Detroit looked on defense early in the season.

"We've cleaned a lot up," Lions head coach Dan Campbell said, via the team's site. "Guys are trusting what they're being taught, they're executing it and with that execution comes confidence."

From ESPN Staff, excerpt from Eric Woodyard:

International games are rare for the Lions, who haven't played outside the U.S. since 2015. And this matchup in Munich is a homecoming of sorts for WR Amon-Ra St. Brown, whose mother is German and who has spent considerable time in the country. This contest also marks Detroit's first game in Germany and the first time it is the designated home team for an international game, so this is certainly a huge deal. "You kind of need that extra story to tell," NFL Germany GM Alexander Steinforth told ESPN. "You need that extra link into the market to have a really meaningful impact, and that's Amon-Ra St. Brown."

The Lions' once-maligned defense has allowed only a total of 62 points during their four-game winning streak. They will get a good test against the Patriots in Munich, Germany, in Week 10, where Jared Goff needs to shake off back-to-back sub 200-yard, one-INT games.

Airport Lounges Are Packed—Travelers Demand $5,000 Annual Credit Card Fees To Keep The Masses Out

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Airport lounges are so crowded that travelers are demanding $200 admission prices and $5,000 annual credit card fees to keep the “untouchables” out, arguing that the “true lounge caste” has been driven into the concourse while everyone else crowds around the buffet. There are also lounges where the line outside and the crowd inside make the benefit nearly worthless. They’re still aspirational to the people getting in, while the people already inside are unhappy that increased access has lowered their relative status. View wyatt.today’s post on Twitter The original poster’s follow-up invokes the tens of thousands of his employer’s dollars he spends on flights, then uses a racial slur for other guests. It’s no longer about finding empty space inside. But most people just want a chance to use the benefit they’re paying for and maybe a snack without standing in line in the terminal like a schmuck to get an $895 ‘free’ drink. Wanting Quiet Space Isn’t Elitist One reply argues that a lounge defeats its purpose when it gets too crowded. It may still have food, a shower, or an airline agent to help when your flight gets delayed or cancels. But the core value proposition is escape from the terminal. I often just want a place to open my laptop without balancing it on my knees. And then having frictionless access to a snack, and the ability to run into the washroom without bringing all my stuff with me, are cherries on top. Nobel laureate James Buchanan’s theory of clubs explains this well. Sharing a facility spreads its costs across more people. Eventually, adding people creates enough congestion to make the experience worse. The membership and the size of the facility have to be considered together. There need to be access limits to preserve the value proposition of the facility. But since most people don’t use the space most of the time, it’s perceived option value that they’re purchasing and it continues to drive premium credit card acquisition and adoption. Lounges Got Better, And People Started Using Them Differently I’ve written about the claim that airport lounge culture represents American decline. The nostaglic view is just wrong. I remember ginger ale and Goldfish crackers, a photocopier in the Admirals Club, and packaged Tillamook cheese in United’s Red Carpet Clubs. Today’s better lounges offer actual meals and craft cocktails. When I visited Chase’s new Dallas–Fort Worth lounge, I found the best food inside a bank lounge that isn’t a Capital One Landing. Plus they have whiskey tastings, and facial treatments. The space is 18,000 square feet. These spaces are no longer just a room with seating, snacks and a fax machine. The same improvements help explain why lounges fill up. People go for the food People who might have stopped in to check e-mail now plan to eat a meal. They come more often and stay longer. A better lounge becomes a destination in its own right. The travel mix has changed. Traditional managed business travel is a smaller part of the story. Premium leisure travelers, families, and people combining work with a trip use the same spaces. A Monday – Thursday consultant arriving just before boarding and a family building in a generous airport buffer spend very different amounts of time. Credit cards have expanded access. That brings in more people, including travelers who wouldn’t buy a separate airline club membership. Uncertain security waits get people to the airport earlier. If security might take ten minutes or an hour, you allow for the hour. When it takes ten minutes, you’ve suddenly got time to spend. Even without an increase in the average security wait, greater uncertainty can mean more time in the airport. I laid out these problems for Delta years ago. Their more substantial food draws people in and keeps them there. And Delta doesn’t just admit customers with its own premium card – American Express Platinum cardmembers flying Delta can enter, too. Platinum’s standard allotment is now 10 visits and Delta Reserve’s is 15, with a spending route to unlimited access. Delta also limits entry before an originating flight to three hours prior to scheduled departure, with an exception for connections. A three-hour entry window still leaves plenty of time for a long visit. And if everyone arrives before the same bank of departures, an empty room earlier in the day doesn’t help. The number of people holding an eligible card isn’t the only number that matters. Double the average length of a visit, with the same flow of arrivals, and you double the average number of people occupying the lounge. I recently catalogued 70 new and expanded lounge projects. More space helps, but a new lounge also attracts people who previously skipped the experience. I’d underscore that which lounges we’re talking about matters a great deal. Spending $895 for a Platinum Card doesn’t on its own signal discernment, although I their Salt Lake lounge is quite nice. Capital One’s Landings at Washington National and LaGuardia offer genuinely good food and reservations. You enter and are shown to a table. That’s a totally different experience. You Could Buy Your Way Into Lounges In The 1960s, Too One commenter remembers the 1990s as a time when people earned their way in by flying. But American Airlines started selling lounge memberships in 1967, at $25 a year or $250 for life. Before that, their sales department handed out memberships. Paid lounge access was a function of the civil rights era – a nondiscriminatory way to determine who could enter. The claim that credit card lounges are bad while airline lounges are good doesn’t make sense. I’d happily choose a good Chase or Capital One lounge over plenty of airline clubs. And finding quiet lounges overseas doesn’t mean most overseas lounges are quiet. Have you ever tried to enter a Priority Pass lounge at Heathrow? Comfort Can Spread Even When Status Can’t The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too. A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people. That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’). I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. When there’s a choice, I’ll pick elsewhere. But some of those Chase (Las Vegas! Phoenix!) and Capital One lounges (Denver!) can make the wait not worth the squeeze, too. Banks are selling more access than they can deliver. Sometimes I’ll just take the restaurant in the terminal. Of course most banks stopped paying for Priority Passs swipes at restaurants – which would be fair enough if they could accommodate travelers in their own lounges. There are also lounges where the line outside and the crowd inside make the benefit nearly worthless. Paying for somewhere comfortable to sit, and then being unable to find a seat, has become a really legitimate complaint – even if it’s mixed with real snobbery because it’s the ‘other’ people who shouldn’t be in there.

“Premium” travel experience before 10am on a Sunday https://t.co/13PniAuksG

— David Slotnick (@David_Slotnick) November 8, 2026

Status anxiety places airline lounges belong alongside BMWs, remote work, and ski trips as things that have lost their cachet as access spread. They’re still aspirational to the people getting in, while the people already inside are unhappy that increased access has lowered their relative status.

The original poster’s follow-up invokes the tens of thousands of his employer’s dollars he spends on flights, then uses a racial slur for other guests. It’s no longer about finding empty space inside. But most people just want a chance to use the benefit they’re paying for and maybe a snack without standing in line in the terminal like a schmuck to get an $895 ‘free’ drink.

One reply argues that a lounge defeats its purpose when it gets too crowded. It may still have food, a shower, or an airline agent to help when your flight gets delayed or cancels. But the core value proposition is escape from the terminal. I often just want a place to open my laptop without balancing it on my knees. And then having frictionless access to a snack, and the ability to run into the washroom without bringing all my stuff with me, are cherries on top. Nobel laureate James Buchanan’s theory of clubs explains this well. Sharing a facility spreads its costs across more people. Eventually, adding people creates enough congestion to make the experience worse. The membership and the size of the facility have to be considered together. There need to be access limits to preserve the value proposition of the facility. But since most people don’t use the space most of the time, it’s perceived option value that they’re purchasing and it continues to drive premium credit card acquisition and adoption. One reply argues that a lounge defeats its purpose when it gets too crowded. It may still have food, a shower, or an airline agent to help when your flight gets delayed or cancels. But the core value proposition is escape from the terminal. I often just want a place to open my laptop without balancing it on my knees. And then having frictionless access to a snack, and the ability to run into the washroom without bringing all my stuff with me, are cherries on top.

Nobel laureate James Buchanan’s theory of clubs explains this well.

Sharing a facility spreads its costs across more people. Eventually, adding people creates enough congestion to make the experience worse. The membership and the size of the facility have to be considered together. There need to be access limits to preserve the value proposition of the facility. But since most people don’t use the space most of the time, it’s perceived option value that they’re purchasing and it continues to drive premium credit card acquisition and adoption. Lounges Got Better, And People Started Using Them Differently I’ve written about the claim that airport lounge culture represents American decline. The nostaglic view is just wrong. I remember ginger ale and Goldfish crackers, a photocopier in the Admirals Club, and packaged Tillamook cheese in United’s Red Carpet Clubs. Today’s better lounges offer actual meals and craft cocktails. When I visited Chase’s new Dallas–Fort Worth lounge, I found the best food inside a bank lounge that isn’t a Capital One Landing. Plus they have whiskey tastings, and facial treatments. The space is 18,000 square feet. These spaces are no longer just a room with seating, snacks and a fax machine. The same improvements help explain why lounges fill up. People go for the food People who might have stopped in to check e-mail now plan to eat a meal. They come more often and stay longer. A better lounge becomes a destination in its own right. The travel mix has changed. Traditional managed business travel is a smaller part of the story. Premium leisure travelers, families, and people combining work with a trip use the same spaces. A Monday – Thursday consultant arriving just before boarding and a family building in a generous airport buffer spend very different amounts of time. Credit cards have expanded access. That brings in more people, including travelers who wouldn’t buy a separate airline club membership. Uncertain security waits get people to the airport earlier. If security might take ten minutes or an hour, you allow for the hour. When it takes ten minutes, you’ve suddenly got time to spend. Even without an increase in the average security wait, greater uncertainty can mean more time in the airport. I laid out these problems for Delta years ago. Their more substantial food draws people in and keeps them there. And Delta doesn’t just admit customers with its own premium card – American Express Platinum cardmembers flying Delta can enter, too. Platinum’s standard allotment is now 10 visits and Delta Reserve’s is 15, with a spending route to unlimited access. Delta also limits entry before an originating flight to three hours prior to scheduled departure, with an exception for connections. A three-hour entry window still leaves plenty of time for a long visit. And if everyone arrives before the same bank of departures, an empty room earlier in the day doesn’t help. The number of people holding an eligible card isn’t the only number that matters. Double the average length of a visit, with the same flow of arrivals, and you double the average number of people occupying the lounge. I recently catalogued 70 new and expanded lounge projects. More space helps, but a new lounge also attracts people who previously skipped the experience. I’d underscore that which lounges we’re talking about matters a great deal. Spending $895 for a Platinum Card doesn’t on its own signal discernment, although I their Salt Lake lounge is quite nice. Capital One’s Landings at Washington National and LaGuardia offer genuinely good food and reservations. You enter and are shown to a table. That’s a totally different experience. You Could Buy Your Way Into Lounges In The 1960s, Too One commenter remembers the 1990s as a time when people earned their way in by flying. But American Airlines started selling lounge memberships in 1967, at $25 a year or $250 for life. Before that, their sales department handed out memberships. Paid lounge access was a function of the civil rights era – a nondiscriminatory way to determine who could enter. The claim that credit card lounges are bad while airline lounges are good doesn’t make sense. I’d happily choose a good Chase or Capital One lounge over plenty of airline clubs. And finding quiet lounges overseas doesn’t mean most overseas lounges are quiet. Have you ever tried to enter a Priority Pass lounge at Heathrow? Comfort Can Spread Even When Status Can’t The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too. A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people. That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’). I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. When there’s a choice, I’ll pick elsewhere. But some of those Chase (Las Vegas! Phoenix!) and Capital One lounges (Denver!) can make the wait not worth the squeeze, too. Banks are selling more access than they can deliver. Sometimes I’ll just take the restaurant in the terminal. Of course most banks stopped paying for Priority Passs swipes at restaurants – which would be fair enough if they could accommodate travelers in their own lounges.
