The same improvements help explain why lounges fill up.

I laid out these problems for Delta years ago. Their more substantial food draws people in and keeps them there. And Delta doesn’t just admit customers with its own premium card – American Express Platinum cardmembers flying Delta can enter, too. Platinum’s standard allotment is now 10 visits and Delta Reserve’s is 15, with a spending route to unlimited access. Delta also limits entry before an originating flight to three hours prior to scheduled departure, with an exception for connections.

A three-hour entry window still leaves plenty of time for a long visit. And if everyone arrives before the same bank of departures, an empty room earlier in the day doesn’t help. The number of people holding an eligible card isn’t the only number that matters. Double the average length of a visit, with the same flow of arrivals, and you double the average number of people occupying the lounge.

I recently catalogued 70 new and expanded lounge projects. More space helps, but a new lounge also attracts people who previously skipped the experience.

I’d underscore that which lounges we’re talking about matters a great deal. Spending $895 for a Platinum Card doesn’t on its own signal discernment, although I their Salt Lake lounge is quite nice. One commenter remembers the 1990s as a time when people earned their way in by flying. But American Airlines started selling lounge memberships in 1967, at $25 a year or $250 for life. Before that, their sales department handed out memberships. Paid lounge access was a function of the civil rights era – a nondiscriminatory way to determine who could enter. And finding quiet lounges overseas doesn’t mean most overseas lounges are quiet. Have you ever tried to enter a Priority Pass lounge at Heathrow? Comfort Can Spread Even When Status Can’t The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too. A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people. That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’). I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. When there’s a choice, I’ll pick elsewhere. But some of those Chase (Las Vegas! Phoenix!) and Capital One lounges (Denver!) can make the wait not worth the squeeze, too. Of course most banks stopped paying for Priority Passs swipes at restaurants – which would be fair enough if they could accommodate travelers in their own lounges. The discussion of aspiration is revealing. Some people feel that accessibility is the inverse of aspiration – that’s true if what you’re aspiring to is being above other people. But you can also just aspire to a more comfortable trip, get it, and be pleased that other people can get it too.

A product can also become less distinctive to its old customers while becoming an attainable aspiration for new ones. The people entering and the people complaining aren’t necessarily the same people.

That’s why the comparison to remote work is revealing – someone getting to work from home doesn’t take away the time you save on your commute working from home. Treating it as a status symbol entails a tradeoff (‘when everyone has status, nobody does’).

I’m glad more people can have a better trip. I also want the benefit I’ve paid for to be usable. I don’t usually bother with Centurion lounges, they aren’t as nice as Capital One and Chase lounges in many airports and there are too many people waiting to get in. When there’s a choice, I’ll pick elsewhere. But some of those Chase (Las Vegas! Phoenix!) and Capital One lounges (Denver!) can make the wait not worth the squeeze, too. Banks are selling more access than they can deliver.

Sometimes I’ll just take the restaurant in the terminal. Of course most banks stopped paying for Priority Passs swipes at restaurants – which would be fair enough if they could accommodate travelers in their own lounges.

Bandai Namco is liquidating Unknown 9: Awakening studio Reflector Entertainment, following multiple previous layoffs since the game's launch

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As another addition to the slew of smaller studios being dissolved by their parent companies, Bandai Namco announced in a press release that they are shutting down their subsidiary studio Reflector Entertainment, a Montreal studio responsible for 2024's action-adventure game Unknown 9: Awakening. In its notice, Bandai Namco explains that the reason for the liquidation is due to Reflector not meeting its expectations for "future business plans, necessary additional investment, and the possibility of profitability and business continuation."

The press release notes that the liquidation of Reflector will result in an estimated "extraordinary loss" of around 21 million euros, or 3.5 billion yen, for Bandai Namco in its Q3 fiscal report of this year. This is not the first time Reflector has been cut by Bandai, as around a month after the launch of Unknown 9: Awakening and presumably in response to its low sales, 18% of the workforce was laid off. 2025 then saw further redundancies amidst the cancellation of a prospective project, though details on those layoffs were not granted by the company. Unknown 9 was intended to be a transmedia franchise involving the game as well as comic books and novels, but these ambitions never came to pass.

Bandai Namco notes that the liquidation will be held to local laws and procedures, which make the actual schedule of the process uncertain for now. A similar process faces Arkane Lyon, which is currently in a purgatory of having been chosen for closure by Microsoft, but temporarily protected by French labor laws that prevent it from being suddenly closed without proper negotiation and procedure.

20 NBA teams' 2026-27 payrolls, from $160 million to more than $254 million

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Spotrac's 2026-27 cap tracker shows 10 of the highest and 10 of the lowest NBA payrolls, and what each total means for roster moves.

Every NBA team builds its roster under rules meant to keep the richest owners from simply buying titles, and the league treats a wide spending gap as a threat to the game itself. "I don't think fans are as enthusiastic about them if one team is spending drastically more than another team," NBA Commissioner Adam Silver told CNBC. The league's strongest answer to that worry is the second apron, a payroll line set so high that a team crossing it gives up much of its freedom to build a roster. Yet the two heaviest-spending teams have already committed money well beyond that line for the 2026-27 season, while the lightest spenders stay close to the salary cap. Who spends the most matters less than what each team gives up by crossing these lines and what it keeps by staying under them.

The lines, more than the raw totals, define where each team stands next season. For 2026-27 the cap sits at $164,961,000, about 6.7% higher than a year earlier, according to Hoops Rumors. Most teams have spent above the cap, so the real differences among them appear in the territory above it. A wide band of luxury-tax territory stretches from the cap to the second apron at $221,686,000, and most of the league operates somewhere inside it. A team past the second apron loses its main tools for fixing a roster. It cannot combine salaries in trades, send out cash in deals or use the mid-level exception, so its large payroll leaves it with fewer options. The cheapest teams have a narrow band of their own to work in, because the league requires every team to spend at least $148,465,000. Even so, a low payroll is not proof of a thrifty team. None of these positions will last beyond one season. The cap is forecast to reach about $174 million in 2027-28, and every team's distance from each line will shift with it.

Spotrac compared how much each NBA team had committed to 2026-27 payroll, from the Phoenix Suns and Los Angeles Lakers to the Los Angeles Clippers and Detroit Pistons. These are promises for a season not yet played, so each total marks where a team starts the season, not where it will finish. The smallest commitment is about $160 million, a few million dollars under the cap, and the largest is more than $254 million, well past the second apron. A gap that wide puts some teams under the league's strictest rules for the entire season and leaves others with nearly every tool the league allows.

The Golden State Warriors have committed more to the 2026-27 season than any other NBA team, and their payroll sits about $89.4 million over the salary cap, according to Spotrac. The Warriors are not just edging past the league's spending lines. They are operating far beyond all of them, so they face every restriction the NBA reserves for its heaviest spenders for the full season. Golden State has the biggest budget in the league, yet that spending leaves it with fewer tools to reshape its roster than teams that spend less.

Golden State is paying for the present and nothing else. The team carries no dead money, so every dollar on its books goes to a player still on the roster. That makes the payroll a deliberate choice about this season rather than a burden left over from past deals. The Warriors have also put most of that money into a few players, with three contracts totaling about $147.1 million. Under the apron limits, that concentration carries real risk. If one of those three players falters, Golden State has few ways to replace what it loses.

So far, the Warriors are winning with the roster they paid for, having opened the season 8-3. Golden State is the clearest example of the trade the league's spending lines force on a team. By spending the most, the Warriors have built a contender, but they have given up the freedom to rebuild it.

For Golden State, the size of the bill matters less than how little room it leaves to change course.

The Minnesota Timberwolves will pay nearly as much as any team in the league next season, and they will spend almost all of it on players who are still on the roster. Minnesota has committed about $252.6 million to its 2026-27 payroll, the second-highest total in the NBA after Golden State, according to Spotrac. Of that sum, the team owes only about $2.1 million to players who have already left. Minnesota is not paying off old trades, buyouts or stars who moved on, so the Timberwolves are paying for the roster they have now.

Minnesota has put much of that money into a few players. The team's three largest active contracts come to about $126.2 million. With so much committed to so few players, Minnesota's season will depend largely on how those players perform rather than on the depth of its bench. That choice also explains why the team's total is so high. Minnesota has to pay a full supporting cast on top of a core that already costs a great deal.

Minnesota has also spent its way well past the league's strictest limit. The second apron for 2026-27 is set at $221.7 million, and Minnesota has committed comfortably more than that, so the Timberwolves will work under the tightest rules the NBA places on roster building. Under those rules, Minnesota cannot easily add new talent to this group. Any change to the roster will have to come from moving salary the team already pays.

The Timberwolves have paid for the team they have, and that is the team they will have to win with.

The Washington Wizards' payroll says more about the freedom the team has given up than about how much it spends. Washington has committed about $243.3 million to 2026-27, the third-highest total in the league, according to Spotrac. Hoops Rumors reports that the league's strictest spending line, the second apron, stands at about $221.7 million for that season. The Wizards are not barely over that line, and a small trim would not bring them back under it. They are well past it, among the teams that have lost the trade and signing tools the league takes from its heaviest spenders. Washington has used up its flexibility along with its money.

None of Washington's spending goes to players who have left. The Wizards list no dead money, which means their limits come from decisions about current players rather than old mistakes. Those decisions center on a few players, and the team's three largest active contracts total about $122.7 million. The Wizards' season depends heavily on whether that small group performs. Past the second apron, the team has few trade routes to rework the group if it falls short. Washington has to make these players succeed rather than replace them.

The Wizards' best chance to get better is the development of players already on the roster, the one kind of improvement the apron rules do not limit. The active roster averages 25 years old. Players that young can still improve, so the team can look for much of its progress from them rather than from trades it can no longer easily make. Washington has paid for patience more than for room to change course, and any improvement will have to come from the players it already has.

Phoenix has committed to one of the league's biggest bills for 2026-27, yet the roster no longer has the stars that bill was built around. The Suns have the fourth-highest commitment, $239,926,801, including $23.2 million in dead money owed to players who are gone, according to Spotrac. Much of that charge is likely the cost of Bradley Beal's exit. The Arizona Republic reports that Phoenix stretched $96 to $97 million of his remaining salary over five seasons. A dead-money charge of that size on the 2026-27 books fits a buyout that comes due year after year. By stretching the salary, the Suns made the yearly cost smaller and the obligation much longer, and they accepted a charge they will carry for seasons in exchange for relief right away.

Cutting Beal bought Phoenix a single season of room to maneuver. With his full salary off the books, the Suns fell below both aprons for 2025-26, the Arizona Republic reports, and the team regained the mid-level exception, access to sign-and-trade deals and the option to pair salaries in a trade. On the 2026-27 books, the Suns have lost that room again, because their commitment sits above that season's $221,686,000 second-apron line. As things stand, they are back past the line that takes away the tools the buyout had restored. Phoenix gained one year of flexibility from the buyout, and the yearly charge it still carries helps keep the payroll high enough to lose that flexibility again.

If the Suns want those tools back, their next move has to cut salary rather than add talent, an odd position for a team with one of the league's biggest bills.

The Nuggets pay as much as almost anyone in the NBA, yet their results fall short of that spending. Denver has committed $235,008,821 to its 2026-27 payroll, the fifth-highest total in the league, according to Spotrac. That total sits above the second apron, so the Nuggets work under the league's strictest spending rules and can do little to reshape the expensive roster they already have. That roster was knocked out of the playoffs by the Minnesota Timberwolves, according to The Gazette. Denver accepted the harshest limits in the league and still did not get past a rival.

Most of Denver's money goes to a few players. The Nuggets pay their three highest-paid players $141,116,779 combined and build the rest of the roster from what remains. Mark Kiszla, a columnist at The Gazette, argues that Jamal Murray, Aaron Gordon and Christian Braun are paid on numbers that Nikola Jokic inflates. If he is right, Denver is paying supporting players for production that comes from its superstar. Murray alone carries a cap hit of about $50 million in 2026-27. By Kiszla's reasoning, much of that salary pays for numbers that depend on playing beside Jokic.

The Nuggets will find this structure hard to change. Past the second apron, they face the league's tightest limits on trades, so any deal to move one of these contracts has to fit inside stricter rules than a team under the line would face. Braun's extension runs five years, and unless Denver finds a trade that works under those rules, that contract will keep the team top-heavy long after next season.

For Denver, the payroll measures how much the team has staked on Jokic lifting the players around him.

The San Antonio Spurs have pushed their payroll into the league's most restricted tier. The team has committed $231,173,962 for 2026-27, the sixth-highest total in the NBA, according to Spotrac. That sum takes San Antonio far past the cap and over the second apron, the league's strictest spending line, which sits at $221,686,000 for that season. The Spurs already work under the limits that come with crossing it. They did not get there by paying for old mistakes, and that makes the next question what they are buying instead.

The Spurs pay only for players on their current roster. The team lists no dead money, and none of its spending covers players who have left. San Antonio also has not piled its payroll onto a few headline deals. Its three largest active contracts total $94,988,300, and the team spends most of its payroll on the rest of the roster. Those players average 25.5 years old, an age at or near a player's prime. For San Antonio, going past the second apron is the price of keeping this group together. The front office chose that limit.

Cleveland is likely to receive San Antonio's first-round pick. That transfer depends on pick swaps being exercised first and is not certain, and the pick is expected to come late in the round, according to Bleacher Report. A late pick means the Spurs are expected to finish with one of the league's better records. The more games San Antonio wins, the less the team gives up by losing that selection.

The Spurs' total marks where a contender stands this season, and what the team can add from here depends on the limits that come with life past the second apron.

The Atlanta Hawks spend near the top of the league without paying a penalty for it. Atlanta has committed about $230 million to 2026-27 payroll, the seventh-highest total in the league, according to Spotrac. Atlanta Hawks On SI reports that the team is still about $3.8 million under the luxury-tax line. Staying under the tax also keeps Atlanta's payroll below both aprons, the higher lines that carry the league's harshest roster rules. The cushion is thin, though. If the Hawks added a single mid-sized contract, they could cross into tax territory, so the front office has to weigh every move against that line.

Atlanta spreads its money across many mid-sized contracts instead of building its books around one star salary. Jalen Johnson is the team's highest-paid player at $30 million, Atlanta Hawks On SI reports, and that is a modest share of a bill this large. The Hawks divide much of the rest among Dyson Daniels, CJ McCollum and a group of players on mid-sized deals. That structure helps a team so close to the tax line, because Atlanta can trade several of those contracts without breaking up its core. Jackson Caudell, a writer for Atlanta Hawks On SI, expects the players moved to be depth pieces such as Corey Kispert, Mouhamed Gueye, Buddy Hield and Ryan Nembhard.

Atlanta's high rank reflects one season more than a lasting level of spending. The Hawks are set to shed more than $60 million in contracts after 2026-27, including those of McCollum, Luguentz Dort and Jock Landale, according to Atlanta Hawks On SI. With those deals ending, the front office could clear financial room next summer or use the contracts as matching salary in a bigger trade, possibly for a star. Neither outcome is certain, and both remain open.

The Hawks' place near the top measures this season's bill, not a plan to stay there, and few teams spending at this level have so much of their future still undecided.

Cleveland has spent just enough to cross into the league's most restrictive spending tier. The Cavaliers have committed $226.1 million to their 2026-27 payroll, the eighth-highest in the league and about $4.4 million past the second apron, according to Spotrac. A trim of a few million dollars would bring them back under that line. Their rank hides how much that small margin matters, because a team just past the second apron faces the same limits as one far past it.

The Cavaliers are paying for the team they have, not for players they have let go. Very little of their 2026-27 total is dead money, and nearly every dollar goes to someone on the current roster. Much of that money goes to a few players. Cleveland's three largest contracts come to about $130.9 million, most of what the salary cap allows for an entire roster. With their books this clean and their core paid this much, the Cavaliers look like a team spending to keep its central players together, not one whose spending spree has gone wrong.

The roster has not yet won enough to match what Cleveland pays for it. The Cavaliers opened this season 5-6, an ordinary record for a team spending near the top of the league. Past the second apron, Cleveland has given up the trade and free-agent tools that teams usually use to fix a slow start. The draft remains the cheapest way for the team to add a player, because a rookie contract carries a low salary. Bleacher Report reports that Cleveland is scheduled to pick late in the first round, though which team's pick it ends up with depends on swaps that have yet to be exercised. That selection would add a real player, though a late first-rounder seldom changes a contender's season.

Cleveland is less a free spender than a team that has paid to keep what it has, and that decision has cost it the freedom to change.

The Knicks carry one of the league's heaviest bills, and they took on almost all of it for players who are still on the roster. New York has committed $223,326,074 to its 2026-27 payroll, the ninth-highest total in the league and enough to put the team past the second apron, according to Spotrac. The Knicks have therefore accepted the strictest limits the league places on any team. They owe only $15,000 to players who have left, which means nearly all of their spending goes to people on the current roster. Their constraint is the price of keeping this team together, not the leftover cost of past mistakes.

New York has built its season around a small core. The Knicks pay their three highest-paid players a combined $137,318,249, most of the team's total, so the season depends heavily on how those three perform. When a team pays that much to so few players, it has to fill out the rest of the roster with whatever money and roster spots remain. Past the second apron, the Knicks will find it hard to upgrade that supporting cast, because they have given up the trade and signing tools that would help them do it.

The Knicks are barely across the second apron. The salaries of their active players fall just below the line, and the team crosses it only when its full total is counted. That thin margin means the team's position can still change. A modest trim would put the Knicks back under the line, and any new salary they take on would move them further past it.

For New York, the payroll rank measures how close the team stands to the league's strictest line, and how little it would take to get back under it.

The Orlando Magic spend enough to stand at the league's strictest line, and it is not clear which side of it they are on. Their 2026-27 commitment of about $222.5 million is the tenth-highest in the NBA and lands just past the second apron, according to Spotrac. Matt Hanifan of Sports Illustrated puts the Magic about $2.5 million below that same line. Both figures rest on the same contracts, since Sports Illustrated draws its contract numbers from Spotrac. The open question for the Magic is which salaries count toward the total.

For the Magic, the second apron's limits are a live question for this season, and a single bench player could decide it. Jamal Cain's $2.6 million salary is not yet guaranteed, according to Sports Illustrated, and that sum is larger than the Magic's margin from the line in either account. The team's choice about Cain, not a star signing, could settle which set of rules it plays under. His contract becomes guaranteed on Jan. 10, so the Magic may not know their place relative to the strictest line until midseason.

Wherever the apron question lands, the Magic are paying a real price for a place near the top of the league's spending. Sports Illustrated reports that a payroll at the second apron line leaves the team with an expected luxury-tax payment north of $32 million. Orlando last paid the tax in 2010-11, so this bill ends a long stretch of staying clear of it and moves the team straight into the league's costliest tier.

For the Magic, the number that matters most is not their total but Jan. 10, the date that will tell the team which side of the line it lives on.

The Detroit Pistons won 60 games last season, according to the Detroit Free Press, yet they head into 2026-27 with one of the smaller bills in the NBA. Spotrac counts about $204.1 million committed for next season, the tenth-lowest total in the league. That low rank does not belong to a weak team cutting costs, and Detroit is not spending cheaply either. Its total still lands well past the salary cap, in the range above the cap where most of the league spends. The Pistons stop short of the first apron, and with little room left beneath that line, it shapes any move they make next.

Detroit had far less money tied to signed players in June than it does now. In June, Detroit had only $135.5 million in active salary for 2026-27, according to Spotrac figures reported by the Detroit Free Press, and that count left out the cap holds teams keep for unsigned free agents. Detroit has since committed about $190.7 million in active salary, most of its total. The low rank no longer describes a roster waiting on its own free agents, because most of the bill now pays players under contract. That makes Detroit's position a real spending level, and it raises the question of why the team stopped where it did.

The answer points to ownership. The Detroit Free Press reports that Pistons owner Tom Gores has not taken the team near the first apron since the league added the second apron in 2023, which leaves the first apron as Detroit's practical hard cap. That restraint has a cost, because any meaningful raise or added salary moves the team toward its owner's limit.

Detroit has shown it can win on a middling bill, and its next real roster move will show whether Gores keeps that limit or the team's success pushes him past it.

The Portland Trail Blazers rank in the cheaper half of the NBA, yet they still spend well past the salary cap. Portland has committed about $203.5 million to 2026-27, the ninth-lowest total in the league, according to Spotrac. That bill puts the Blazers about $38.6 million over the cap, which means they cannot sign free agents into open space. They have not reached the tax either. Sports Illustrated reports that heading into free agency the team had about $30.8 million of room under the luxury tax. Portland sits between those two lines, and its position between them says more about what the team can do than its rank does.

Because they are over the cap, the Blazers can sign outside free agents only through exceptions. Their biggest such tool is the mid-level exception for teams that stay under the tax, and it carries a value of $15 million, according to Sports Illustrated. That is enough for a useful rotation player, not a star. The team's two notable free agents, Matisse Thybulle and Robert Williams III, are not under contract, so the committed total does not yet include them. Whatever Portland spends to keep or replace them will come out of the same room under the tax.

Sports Illustrated reports that Blazers owner Tom Dundon has been hesitant to spend and wants to avoid a tax bill. If he holds to that, the room between the current bill and the tax may be close to the team's entire budget for improving the roster this summer.

Portland's summer will turn on how much of its room under the tax Dundon agrees to spend.

The Lakers spend less than most of the league on next season, and they are still winning. Spotrac reports that the team has committed about $200.9 million to 2026-27, the eighth-lowest total in the league, and the Lakers opened this season 10-2. A lower-third payroll in this league still sits far above the cap, and the Lakers' total runs about $35.9 million past it. They stop short of the first apron, though, so none of the stricter limits on how a team builds its roster apply to them yet. The Lakers have room left to spend, not a budget they have chosen to hold.

Almost all of that money pays for players the Lakers actually have, with only about $4.2 million of the total going to dead money owed to players who are gone. The roster is still taking shape. Its future rests on Luka Doncic, who is on the 2026-27 books, while LeBron James is unsure whether he will play beyond this season, according to CNBC. The team is changing in the owner's box as well as on the court.

Decisions about how far toward the aprons to go now belong to Mark Walter, the Lakers' new majority owner, who took control in a sale that valued the team at $10 billion, according to CNBC. Jeanie Buss, the Lakers governor and former controlling owner, tied the handoff directly to money. "What was important to him was that the Lakers stay at the top of the NBA, and to stay at the top of the NBA, you need to have the resources," Buss said. By her account, the sale was about having resources. That purpose belongs to a team whose commitment for next season still ranks in the lower third of the league.

The Lakers sit near the bottom because of money they haven't spent yet, and the people on both sides of the sale made spending it the point of the deal.

On paper, the Clippers look like one of the league's more restrained teams, but in this league restraint still means paying far past the salary cap. The Clippers have committed about $200.3 million to 2026-27, the seventh-lowest total in the league and still about $35.3 million beyond the cap, according to Spotrac. That total stays below the first apron, so on the books the Clippers have crossed neither of the league's two strictest lines and look freer than the heavy spenders above them. That picture leaves out the penalties the league has just handed the team.

The league has also punished the Clippers in ways their payroll total does not show. ABC News reports that the NBA fined the franchise $30 million and took away five draft picks. The team pays that fine while it still sits under the first apron. The lost picks may cost the Clippers more over time, because draft picks are the assets a team uses to add players or make trades. The league punished the Clippers for working around the cap rules themselves, so their place below the apron says nothing about whether they followed those rules. "I am deeply disappointed by the flagrant violations of our rules and by the Clippers' institutional and leadership failures that led to this misconduct," NBA Commissioner Adam Silver said.

The roster itself is not settled. The Clippers' trade of Kawhi Leonard to the Toronto Raptors is on hold until the investigation is resolved, according to ABC News, and the team could still lose its star and change shape before the season starts. The Clippers have also said they will challenge the penalty through arbitration.

Until the Leonard trade either goes through or falls apart, the Clippers' payroll tells a reader little about the team they will field.

Milwaukee has one of the league's smaller bills for 2026-27, but a large part of it pays players who no longer play for the Bucks. The team has committed about $192.4 million, the sixth-lowest total in the league, and roughly $27.4 million of that is dead money owed to departed players, according to Spotrac. The Bucks' freedom to spend depends on those payments. Even with a low rank, Milwaukee sits over the cap with no space at all, and the amount by which it exceeds the cap is almost exactly what it still owes those former players. Without the old obligations, the Bucks would be right around the cap line. With them, the team has no room to sign players outright. Their modest total reflects past contracts, not a choice to save money.

Being over the cap means the Bucks cannot sign outside free agents into open space, and any change to the roster has to come through trades or exceptions. Those routes matter to a team whose current roster has struggled, opening the season 1-9. The front office has a clear reason to reshape the team and no cap room to do it with.

For the Bucks, the payments to players who have left matter more than the modest total, because those payments are why a low-spending team has no free hand.

A payroll near the bottom of the league does not mean a team is under the salary cap, and the Jazz are an example. Utah has committed $190,850,101 to 2026-27, the fifth-lowest payroll in the NBA, according to Spotrac. That bill still puts the Jazz $25,889,101 over the cap, so they have no open room to sign free agents outright. Utah ranks this low because teams across the league have spent far past the cap, not because the Jazz have held back.

None of Utah's payroll goes to players who have left, because the Jazz carry no dead money. Utah has put most of its payroll into a small group, and the three biggest contracts on the roster total $109,792,166. Utah's direction depends more on whether those three players can lead a winning team than on the players around them.

Being over the cap still leaves the Jazz their trade tools, because their payroll sits below the first apron. None of the limits that come with the aprons apply to Utah. The roster on those books is not winning yet, with a 1-9 record early in the season. A losing team needs ways to change, and the Jazz still have them. They can trade without the restrictions the heaviest spenders face.

Utah's payroll is a starting point for a different roster, not a commitment the team cannot get out of.

The Charlotte Hornets are one of the league's cheapest teams, but they still have no money free to spend. Charlotte has committed about $173.2 million to the 2026-27 season, the fourth-lowest total in the NBA, according to Spotrac. A team that far down the payroll ladder would seem to have room to add players. Yet the Hornets are about $8.2 million over the salary cap, so they have no cap space to sign free agents outright. Charlotte is only a little less boxed in than the teams that spend more, and it has no cushion under the cap at all.

The Hornets are over the cap because they still pay for players who have left. The salaries of Charlotte's active players come to about $161.7 million, which is less than the cap, so the team would sit under the line if it paid only its current roster. The team also owes about $9.0 million in dead money on contracts for players no longer on the roster, and that sum is larger than its overage. Without that old bill, Charlotte would have cap room this summer. The Hornets are not a cheap team that chose to stop at the cap. They have a modest roster and an old debt, and they are giving up flexibility this season to pay that debt.

The number that decides how freely Charlotte can move is not what it pays its players but what it still owes the ones who left.

Teams that spend little usually keep room to add talent, but the Bulls have none. Chicago has committed $165,770,083 to 2026-27, the third-lowest total in the league, according to Spotrac. That total is still just barely over the $164.96 million salary cap. A team below the cap can sign a free agent outright with the space it has left. The Bulls have given up that option by a small margin, but they have kept almost everything else. The first apron is set at $209,015,000, far above Chicago's total, so the Bulls keep the trade and exception tools that the league's heaviest spenders lose.

Chicago spends nearly all its money on the players it puts on the floor. The Bulls carry $1,075,459 in dead money, a trivial sum on a bill this size, so almost none of their spending covers players who have left. The Bulls are over the cap because of the roster they chose to keep. The question for Chicago is not what old deals cost the team but whether this group of players is worth paying at the cap.

The early results give no sign yet that it is. The Bulls opened the season 1-8, and a stretch that short is a warning rather than a verdict. Still, a losing team with no cap space can't fix its roster by signing free agents. For Chicago, trades are the realistic route to change.

The Bulls' total marks neither a thrifty team nor an ambitious one, but a team that has kept its tools and must decide whether to use them on this roster or a different one.

The Memphis Grizzlies have committed less to next season than almost any team in the league, yet they have only a little room left under the salary cap. Their 2026-27 commitment of $161.1 million is the second-lowest in the NBA and keeps them a few million dollars under the cap, according to Spotrac. Memphis spends little without having much left to spend, and the reason lies in whom the team is paying.

The Grizzlies owe a large share of that total to players who no longer play for them. About $40.6 million is dead money, so the roster Memphis sends onto the court costs far less than its headline total. The small figure therefore does not show a team that chose a lean budget. It shows a team that keeps paying for past decisions while it fields an inexpensive roster. Memphis's thin margin under the cap is what remains after it covers those old obligations. Despite spending so little, the team still has little room to add talent.

The players Memphis does pay are early in their careers, and the roster's average age of 24.4 reflects a team built on young players rather than costly veterans. That young group has started well, opening the 2025-26 season with a 7-3 record. Ten games settle little about how good the Grizzlies are, but they show that the team behind this low payroll is competing.

Memphis is carrying old bills while its young core wins, with enough room to stay under the cap and little to add to a group that is working.

The Brooklyn Nets have the lowest payroll in the NBA, yet they have little room left under the salary cap. Brooklyn has committed $160,056,358 to 2026-27, the lowest total in the league, and that leaves the team about $4.9 million of cap space, according to Spotrac. That much space could pay for a modest contract but not a major free agent. Brooklyn has kept its books clean, with no dead money at all. Every dollar of the bill pays a player on the current roster. The low total reflects the team Brooklyn puts on the floor, and past contracts for departed players play no part in it.

The Nets spend most of their budget on a few players. Brooklyn's three highest-paid players account for about $89.6 million, and the rest of the roster shares what remains. That roster has an average age of 24.1, so the Nets are paying mostly for players in the first years of their careers. With so little cap space, Brooklyn cannot add much talent from outside this summer. The team's direction depends on which of its current players develop and whether the three players at the top of its payroll prove worth that money. The front office will judge this young group before it commits large new sums to any of them.

Brooklyn has shed every old obligation and built around youth, but it still has only a sliver of cap room to work with.

Why Daniel Radcliffe May Not Watch HBO's Harry Potter TV Series

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Will Daniel Radcliffe watch the new Harry Potter TV show on HBO with his family? It seems like he'll be skipping it.

"I don't know if I will [watch], honestly," Radcliffe told the Associated Press (via Deadline). "I've watched, I've been in a lot of Harry Potter already ... and also I don't watch any TV."

That latter claim seems unlikely when you consider that Radcliffe has appeared in a handful of really good TV shows, including one that's running now and happens to be one of the best comedy shows of the year. He brought receipts for this claim, though. "I haven't seen 'Breaking Bad.' I haven't seen 'Mad Men.' I haven't seen some of the like the real classics. So, you know, eight hours is a lot of anything to watch."

Let's be fair: If Radcliffe is being truthful here, he does have some important homework to do. (If he really hasn't watched any television, I'd add some other picks to his queue like "Veep," "Succession," "Vanderpump Rules," and "Fleabag," just to cover a few vital bases.) This also does line up with Radcliffe's apparent attempts, over the years, to distance himself from the wizarding world that made him famous; for those who may not know, the actor played the titular Harry Potter in the original film franchise. In February of 2026, Radcliffe told Parade he wouldn't consider returning to the Harry Potter universe in any way:

"[Potter] was great to do, but I don't want to go back to it. Honestly ... I feel like, in the same way that I feel about like all the jobs I've done, I loved doing them, and I hold the memories of them very, very precious and treasure them, but like I wouldn't want to go back tomorrow ... There's a lot more to do."

In that same conversation with the Associated Press, Daniel Radcliffe was asked if he has advice for the new actor playing the Boy Who Lived in HBO's series, which aims to devote one season each to all seven books from the original novel series. (The first season, accordingly, is titled "Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone.") Dominic McLaughlin will portray Harry; he's joined by Alastair Stout and Arabella Stanton taking over the roles of Harry's two best friends, Ron Weasley and Hermione Granger, from actors Rupert Grint and Emma Watson.

Radcliffe demurred yet again. "I'm really truly in the business of not giving Dominic advice. He's got it. He doesn't need any advice from me," Radcliffe said, pretty reasonably. "If I say something to you, then the next interview people have with him, they'll be like, 'Oh, and Dan said you should do this,' and I'm like, I don't want to haunt this poor child through interviews."

The Tony winner continued: "So I have no advice for him. He's going to be amazing, and you know, I hope that I will, I think I will, get to meet him someday — and I'm very excited about that."

So what has Radcliffe been up to since he hung up his Gryffindor cloak and put away his magic wand for good? Quite a lot, actually!

People can get pigeonholed into roles, and being a child star is exceptionally difficult. Somehow, Daniel Radcliffe managed to overcome both of those hurdles and become one of Hollywood and Broadway's most exciting and audacious performers. (I say "somehow" even though Radcliffe has been pretty open about how his parents helped shield him from some of the most difficult parts of fame at a young age — but still, it's impressive!)

Even during the original run of the Harry Potter films, Radcliffe worked extensively on stage in daring projects like "Equus" (a seriously dark drama that requires full nudity and allowed Radcliffe to act alongside Sir Richard Griffiths, who played his evil Uncle Vernon in the Potter films) and "How to Succeed in Business Without Really Trying." Despite his apparent aversion to television, Radcliffe has also turned in excellent performances on shows like "A Young Doctor's Notebook" (where, hilariously, he plays a younger version of "Mad Men" star Jon Hamm) and "Miracle Workers," and as far as films go, he's pushed himself in comedies like "Weird" and "The Lost City."

I'm most excited, though, about Radcliffe's starring role alongside Tracy Morgan on "The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins." As Morgan's former football player Reggie tries to restore his reputation and potentially get voted into the NFL Hall of Fame, Radcliffe's Oscar-winning documentarian Arthur Tobin chronicles his life ... and the two are a perfect pair. I hope that Radcliffe has at least watched Morgan on "30 Rock" — but in any case, you can stream "Reggie Dinkins" on Peacock now, and "Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone" premieres on HBO on Christmas Day.

The Impact of a Psychopathic Mother

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 10, 2026 | Reviewed by Michelle Quirk

When my children were young, I remember reading aloud children’s books that delighted both them and me. One book that especially comes to mind is Diane Goode’s Where’s Our Mama? 1 The author dedicated this book to her own mother, and her feelings for her mother are expressed through the two young children in the story. In the story, a gust of wind blew the mother’s hat off her head. When the mother went to fetch her hat, the two children became separated from her and began to cry. A policeman approached them as they searched for their mama and asked them to describe her. They quickly said she was the most beautiful and bravest person who always wears the prettiest hats, has a voice that everyone loves, and is the best cook in the world. Of course, they were all soon joyfully reunited.

As in the story, “mama” elicits an image of a loving, giving, selfless person. But some people never had such a mother. Many were born the children of psychopathic mothers. For those in such circumstances, what can they expect?

According to psychopathy experts, Paul Babiak and Robert D. Hare, in their book, Snakes in Suits, “Psychopaths may push the envelope of acceptable human behavior … . Some psychopaths live in society and do not technically break the law—although they may come close, with behavior that usually is very unpleasant for those around them. Some may lead seemingly normal lives, not hurting people in ways that attract attention, but causing problems nonetheless in hidden economic, psychological, and emotionally abusive ways. They do not make warm and loving parents, children, or family members.”2

Having had a mother and sister who were highly psychopathic, I had eye-opening experiences from when I was young. I noticed early on that both my mother and sister were different from other mothers and other children that I knew. But it took years before I was able to understand the term that described their personality disorder. Their behavior displayed the psychopathic traits of lovelessness and guiltlessness, which sociologists William and Joan McCord have characterized as fundamental to the disorder.3

In realizing this, I came to terms with the fact that my mother was the antithesis of the iconic mother. This realization has helped me to make sense of much of what I experienced and observed growing up. She consistently exhibited strange and unusual behavior. Previously, I could never understand why she hid and disregarded treasured family photographs. One day, I happened to find them hidden in a dark, clammy basement behind a piece of plywood on a cold, damp cement floor. They had not been placed there by accident. The age-old photographs were damaged by mildew and could not be replaced. When I asked her about it, she shrugged and was expressionless, showing her “I don’t care” attitude. She had no affection or regard for the warm memories the photos embodied. Instead, photographs of complete strangers were showcased in prominent places in the house for no apparent reason.

My mother had many strategies to get her way by creating conflicts and division between family members and relatives. I witnessed these conflicts all my life at different times and in many situations. One day, I happened to call my mother, only to find out that my niece had recently been married. My mother’s voice could not have sounded more delighted to tell me that I was the only person not invited. She perpetuated ill will and division. According to psychologist Neville Symington, “One of the most evident signs of psychopathy is the presence of confusion and bad feeling. One person is set against another and suspicion is rife, but the cause is never rooted out.”4

The brush-offs and the slights were countless, and the cold treatment never changed, even during a time of great personal distress. In such a time, even a stranger can sympathize with someone going through hardship. Instead, my mother and sister went to an astrologer to find out if my life would be ruined and, on a telephone call, they blurted out in high-pitched laughter exactly what the future had in store for me. They tried to get me to believe I would be ruined. I didn’t believe, and it never occurred, but their diabolical scheming was another sign of their disorder.

In dreaming of what a mother is, I came to grips with the fact that I never had a mother like "mama," and that my mother could never be trusted.

1. Goode, Diane. (1991). Where's Our Mama? New York, NY: Dutton Children's Books.

2. Babiak, Paul & Hare, Robert D. (2007). Snakes in Suits: When Psychopaths Go to Work. New York, NY: Collins Business. 19–20.

3. McCord, W. & McCord J. (1964). The Psychopath: An Essay on the Criminal Mind. Princeton, NJ: Van Nostrand. 13, 16.

4. Symington, Neville. (2001). The Response Aroused by the Psychopath. In Meloy, J. Reid (Ed.), The Mark of Cain: Psychoanalytic Insight and the Psychopath. (pp. 283–296). Hillsdale, NJ: The Analytic Press. 290.

Winifred Rule is a member of the Society for the Scientific Study of Psychopathy and author of Born to Destroy, the first instructional book on the female psychopath, based on her experiences and lessons learned from living with two psychopaths.

Why Everyone Is Suddenly Talking About Van Deer Skis

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

Backed by Red Bull and fresh off Paula Moltzan’s first World Cup win, Van Deer is turning up in shops across North America. Here’s what we know about the skis, the price and the market. (Photo: Getty Images)

Overview: Van Deer is a ski brand created by racing legend Marcel Hirscher and majority-owned by Red Bull. Launched in 2021, it makes World Cup race, groomer, freeride and touring skis, priced from about $1,000 to $1,700. Paula Moltzan won her first World Cup race on Van Deer skis in Sölden. Distribution in North America is still limited, but the brand plans to open more storefronts this year.

If you watched the opening women’s World Cup GS in Sölden, Austria, you probably did a double take at Paula Moltzan’s skis. The U.S. racer won by 0.01 for her first career World Cup victory, and she did it on Van Deer-Red Bull Sports, a nascent brand she switched to this season. Van Deer is the brainchild of ski-racing GOAT Marcel Hirscher, and Moltzan’s win brought instant attention to a brand with a unique origin story.

The brand launched in 2021 and gained notoriety in 2022 with Norwegian Henrik Kristoffersen’s success on its skis, including a slalom crystal globe, an Olympic medal and a World Championship gold. Now Van Deer skis are suddenly turning up everywhere in places like Deer Valley and Vail—and even at big retailers like Evo. They’re not cheap: prices run from about $1,000 to $1,700, but it seems skiers are willing to pay. “Last year we had a phenomenal year with Van Deer skis,” said Billy Schoff, equipment buyer at Gorsuch, a high-end ski shop, which has sold them for three seasons. “We get people out on them once; they love them and then the word spreads like wildfire.”

So what is Van Deer, who are its skis for, and where does it fit in the North American market? Here’s what to know.

The details of Van Deer are deeply woven into what’s meaningful to Hirscher, who originally retired in 2019 (he came out of retirement in 2024, only to tear his ACL and be sidelined again). The brand’s name is a witty wordplay on his heritage—he’s half Dutch, half Austrian, and the German word “hirsch” means deer, hence: Van Deer. The H-Power 68 ski model nods to Hirscher’s Project 68, his initiative to get another win after his 67 career World Cup wins, even if he’s not the one racing anymore.

As a startup ski brand, it’s incredibly difficult to compete in a saturated ski market full of legacy brands, but along with Hirscher’s passion and precision, Van Deer has something other brands struggle with—resources. Hirscher was a Red Bull-sponsored athlete and maintains a strong relationship with the company. The partnership with Red Bull Sports, a multi-billion-dollar global brand, affords Van Deer-Red Bull Sports the ability to build their own factory stocked with state-of-the-art machines, hire top people to work for them, and support top athletes like Moltzan. (Red Bull has a 51 percent majority stake in Van Deer.)

“Marcel is obsessed with building the best-quality and best-selling skis,” said Schoff, who along with Dave Gorsuch was brought in early on Hirscher’s project. Moltzan sees the Red Bull effect from the athlete side: “Van Deer is still a new brand, so having Red Bull behind it makes a big difference. They provide a lot of support, which lets us focus on building the best skis we can.”

From the outside, Moltzan’s move looked like a risk. She had skied for Rossignol for 18 years and was coming off a season with four second-place World Cup finishes and an Olympic medal. “I know the change might look risky from the outside, but I was ready for it,” she said. “I was looking for new motivation and inspiration, and the chance to work with a team that has the ability and resources to build equipment specifically for me was a huge draw.”

She didn’t have a direct conversation with Hirscher before making the switch—they chatted briefly when she arrived at the factory. “I was going to be the only woman on the skis, and I knew there was a lot of work ahead of us, but that was also part of what made it exciting,” she said. “The first time I got on them, they felt completely different from anything I’d skied before, but I could immediately tell they were going to be good.”

Her husband and longtime serviceman, Ryan Mooney, was sold at the factory. “Ryan was really impressed when we toured the factory, the level of precision during the build process is certainly something that sets the brand apart from others,” Moltzan said. “He thought the powerful nature of the ski could be a great match for my style of skiing.”

True to its racing roots, every Van Deer ski has a World Cup construction and finish, but the brand now goes well beyond the race course with wider waist widths. Today there are five series:

Van Deer’s consumer skis are all unisex for now. On the race side, Moltzan says Van Deer didn’t start from an existing ski. “We’ve built completely new skis for the women’s side, so my feedback has been part of the process from the beginning,” she said, along with input from Mooney and Berni Arnitz, her second technician, who oversees women’s development. What makes them different? “Women’s skis are different from men’s skis, including the length, shape, and construction,” she said. “But I don’t think it needs to be more complicated than that.”

For now, finding the right ski comes down to length and model. “There are shorter sizes like 147 in H-Power 68 and 159 in H-Power 78,” says Max Wingard, Van Deer-Red Bull Sports race and promotions manager. “The SL model is the ski we’d sell as an easier to ski slalom ski. The GS Pro could be a masters ski, but comes in longer lengths.”

We haven’t had a chance to test the skis ourselves yet, but according to those who’ve tried them, they’re not just for racers. “I think the best customer for Van Deer is someone who really loves skiing and cares about their equipment,” Moltzan said. “You don’t have to be a racer. There are skis for everything from race courses to touring, but they’re built for people who want high performance and appreciate the details that go into a great pair of skis.”

Eric Lipton, National Alpine Team alumnus and director of Destination Ski Camps, skied the HP-78 and described it as “predictable and stable at speed.” “I’d describe the skis as having a race ski heart and soul in a consumer package,” he said.

The North American hardgoods market is a different animal from Europe’s piste-focused market, and Van Deer is making a clear play for more traction in the U.S. According to Schoff, the brand plans to build a North American warehouse, and it has recently hired George Couperthwait, formerly of Stöckli, to help build the brand and its dealer network here.

Schoff sees the target clearly. “I spent time in Austria with Leon (Hirscher’s brother, formerly of Atomic who now works on product for Van Deer) and his father, Ferdinand,” he said. “They were obsessed with building unbelievable race products and now the obsession includes commercial skis. They are doing it the old craftsman way, but with new machines that put on the best finish and even rip the sidewalls back. The quality of construction and finish is top quality and it’s clear they are going after high-end brands like Stöckli.”

Distribution is still limited, says Wingard, but the goal this year is to open more storefronts and grow the relationship with their existing retailers as well.

Wingard, who previously worked for Marker Völkl Dalbello and Stöckli, thinks Van Deer will catch on in both the East and the West. The line includes multiple narrow-waisted skis tailor-made for East Coast conditions. Out West, Van Deer will be pushing the Freeride 98 and 108, with former World Cup racer brand ambassador Travis Ganong giving feedback on the series and stores like Gorsuch in Vail, Aspen and Park City on board.

As a ski coach and former racer, I have a fondness for racey skis in a consumer package. You can feel the high-end construction and finish right out of the box, even in the way the bases glide on snow. A close comparison is Stöckli. For years, Stöckli skis have been a favorite among ski instructors, because skiers who focus on carving, like me, tend to gravitate toward skis with rebound energy, edge grip and stability. Van Deer shares Stöckli’s high cost and a degree of exclusivity, but the top-grade race materials and hand-tuning steps are meant to justify the price.

Van Deer’s advantage is that it has pulled together European craftsmanship, enticing long-time legacy-brand employees to come aboard, with state-of-the-art ski technology like the Wintersteiger Jupiter tuning machine and a brand-new factory, thanks to Red Bull’s resources.

All models except the Freeride 108 (which has some carbon) and the Touring series have a game-on construction: a double layer of Titanal and a woodcore of poplar, ash or beech. The Van Deer models available this season best suit strong intermediate to expert skiers who can drive a ski through a turn transition and want the ski to initiate a turn without too much effort, while still feeling edge grip underfoot and acceleration out of the turn.

You don’t have to ski like Paula Moltzan to appreciate a pair of Van Deers, but perhaps you aspire to turn like she does.

Current models are available online as well as at speciality retailers. Skiers interested in demoing or buying a pair can find a dealer on the company website. As for Moltzan, she’s racing around the globe, with Levi, Finland, next, followed by Killington, Vermont. As good as the skis already are, she says, they’re still a work in progress: “Skis are always evolving, so there will definitely be more changes to come.”

Take-Two Boss Says Ocarina Of Time Is “Terrific,” But Is It Competing With GTA 6?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Take-Two boss Strauss Zelnick has reacted to the release of Nintendo's recent Switch 2 remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. He said the game is "terrific" and that Nintendo should be "very proud of the title."

But is it a competitor to GTA 6, which hails from Take-Two's Rockstar Games? Zelnick told GamesBeat that in the entertainment business, "You compete against everything and nothing."

Everything, he said, because consumers have so many choices about how to spend their time and money. And nothing because entertainment is not "must-have" like gas or groceries, so people will only buy if they feel compelled to.

"So it’s our job to make the best entertainment on Earth. When we do that, people will show up for it," he said.

This isn't the first time that the Ocarina of Time remake has been brought up in a conversation about the biggest games of 2026. Activision marketing chief Tyler Bahl recently said GTA 6 and Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4 are the two biggest games of 2026, not Zelda. "No offense to Zelda," he said.

The Ocarina of Time remake is doing great so far in terms of review scores, currently standing as the highest-rated game of 2026. The game's sales numbers haven't been divulged yet, but its release is helping Switch 2 hardware sales.

As for GTA 6, the game launches on November 19 for PS5 and Xbox Series X|S. You can also stream it to your Xbox One as part of Microsoft's new 10-year deal with Take-Two.

How Spanish Media Feel About Julian Alvarez After Failed Arsenal Move

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It was perhaps the biggest saga of the summer: Julian Alvarez had his heart set on leaving Atletico Madrid, but he was unable to secure a move away.

The Argentine's preference was Barcelona, but his club were unwilling to let him move to their La Liga rivals. The forward's desire to leave was so public – as was Barca's interest – Atletico even released an explosive statement, insisting he was not for sale:

“The player is being victimised, but the only victim of the Julian case is us. They have harmed us economically and socially.

“There is zero per cent chance of selling him to Barca. This situation is not about money, it’s about dignity. There is zero per cent chance of selling it to Barca."

With Arsenal in the market for a forward, the Gunners were ready to land Alvarez if he gave up on his Barcelona dream. However, the Argentine didn't seem open to leaving Spain and with Atletico true to their word, he was not sold.

The summer transfer window closed two months ago. Since then, Alvarez has slowly been reintegrated back into the team. Here's a look at how he's getting on.

When the 2026/27 season started, the transfer market was still open. This made for an extremely messy opening few games. Although Alvarez missed Atletico's opening La Liga game, he was involved in their next game at home against Villarreal.

He played 24 minutes off the bench in a 2-2 draw at the Metropolitano Stadium, and was booed by his own fans when entering the pitch. There were even reports that club CEO Miguel Angel Gil Marin insisted that the Argentine feature, as some kind of punishment, knowing that he would be jeered by the Atletico faithful.

It was suggested that this 'humiliation therapy' would force Alvarez to accept an offer from Arsenal, thus allowing Atleti to cash in on the clearly unhappy player, but not to Barcelona. However, the transfer deadline passed and no move occurred.

Since then, Alvarez has played 11 more games for his side, with four coming in the Champions League. He has just one goal and three assists, with seven starts in a total of 12 appearances.

He played the full 90 minutes in the recent home loss to Barcelona, picking up an assist. Following the defeat, the Spanish media made their feelings on the player very clear.

The La Liga side are paying some of their players very handsomely

Although he picked up an assist for Giuliano Simeone's goal, Alvarez also missed two clear chances. These included a one-on-one with Barca goalkeeper Joan Garcia, as well as a moment where he skied a big opportunity when through on goal again.

The Spanish press, via Goal, were not impressed. Journalist Manu Carreno was particularly sharp on Cadena SER radio:

"He is still unable to prove that he is worth 100 or 150 million euros. Atletico needs a striker who decides matches."

Former player Kiki Narvaez highlighted missed chances, saying: "They are two very clear chances, to the point that there was not even any reason to shout."

El Pais journalist Ladislao Monino said: "Given his technical abilities, it is not normal for Julian Alvarez to waste two chances as clear as that, as he did." While analyst Nacho Valencia noted on Radio Marca:

"You leave feeling like you threw your money in the bin."

With just months to go until the January transfer market opens, it will be interesting to see if Alvarez pushes to leave Atletico in the winter. If so, it remains to be seen if he only has eyes for Barcelona once more, or if a move to Arsenal could be on the cards.

Don't throw away your old phone box — you might need it in an emergency

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

That cardboard box in your closet can come in handy more often than you’d think.

There's a specific type of anxiety that comes with deciding what to do with a box. Do you immediately throw it away? Do you stash it in a closet with about 20 other boxes that store little more than your own guilt? If you chuck it, you might need it for some unknown purpose in the future. But if you don't, that box might just sit in your home indefinitely.

It doesn't help when it's a nice box, either, like the type your phone might have arrived in. It's likely made of thick cardboard, with convenient compartments. Luckily, or perhaps unluckily for those who have finally gathered the nerve to go on a recycling tear, there are a few ways to help you decide the fate of your phone's box. Whether you're ready to resell your phone or need to report a stolen one, here's why you might want to keep your phone's box.

Hopefully, you and your phone will never part ways without your consent. However, if it does get stolen, you'll need its IMEI (International Mobile Equipment Identity) number. An IMEI is a 15- 17-digit number unique to your phone. Carriers can use it to identify the device and block it if it's lost or stolen. AT&T describes it as your phone's "fingerprint."

One place to check for your IMEI number is the box your phone came in. You should also be able to find it through your Apple or Google account, depending on the device you have. Still, in the heat of the moment, grabbing that box from a drawer might be the easiest option.

Some less stressful scenarios might also lead you to keep your phone box. If you purchased an iPhone from Apple directly, for example, you have 14 days to return it from the day you received it. It must also be in the original packaging. Likewise, Samsung requires original packaging. Other retailers might have different policies, so at the very least, it's a good idea to check before you trash the box. Or keep it until after the window has closed, just to be safe.

Additionally, if you're thinking of reselling your phone at some point down the line, having its original box could come in handy. For one, you might have saved additional cables or something like a SIM ejector tool that a new owner will want. And while there's no hard data, some experts believe that having that original box can boost your odds of selling your phone at a good price.

Consumer Reports suggests the box increases the market value of the item and shows that you've taken good care of it. If you saw two identical iPhones for the same price, but only one was in a box, would you really choose the one without the box? The article also notes that you'll already have a box if you need to ship your phone.

So, if you're waffling on the eternal question of whether to save your phone's box, you can consider if any of those situations apply to you. And if not, there's likely a recycling bin somewhere waiting for its next deposit.

The coolest ride in town is England’s first electric ice cream truck

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The “world’s leading ice cream truck manufacturer” has released its first fully electric ice cream truck, which is already in operaiton, and it says it’s open for orders for more.

Whitby Morrison bills itself as “the world’s leading ice cream truck manufacturer,” supplying converted vans as ice cream trucks to drivers worldwide.

And now it has taken a big step into the modern age delivering its first fully electric ice cream truck to Hocking’s dairy in North Devon, England.

The truck has been operating since July, through the course of one “ice cream season.” It reportedly covered more than 100 miles a day and has done days serving up to 12 hours at a time.

It’s built on a standard Mercedes eSprinter, so it’s not retrofitted from the diesel vans that make up most of Whitby Morrison’s other offerings. The eSprinter comes in a “chassis cab” configuration which comes with just the chassis and cab, and lets upfitters build whatever they want onto the back end of the vehicle.

In this case, it also got some pretty extensive (and a little over-the-top) art all around the vehicle, highlighting the van’s electric operation.

The ice cream truck maker, Whitby Morrison, says this is the “world’s first fully certified electric ice cream truck.”

We’re not sure what sort of international certifications are required for an ice cream truck so we’ll take their word for it, but we have seen a Rivian EDV turned into a Ben & Jerry’s ice cream truck, complete with side window, and a concept electric ice cream truck as far back as 2019. So it’s certainly not the very first time this idea has been had, at least.

And it is a good idea. As with school buses, ice cream trucks are vehicles that children are likely to congregate around. Having a stinky old truck spewing emissions just a few feet away from those kids’ lungs is the sort of thing that can expose them to a life of health problems… and we might as well not stack that on top of the sugar addiction we’re teaching them to have (hey, at least they have to get outside to chase the truck down, so they’re getting some physical activity out of it, right?)

It also opens up more possibilities for operators to serve at events where having a diesel truck running might not be reasonable, like in enclosed spaces or where quiet might be appreciated (this came up when we went to Harbinger Motors’ factory opening, where local food trucks were in attendance, inside the large factory building, and made the air quality noticeably worse).

Whitby Morrison says it’s taking orders both in the UK and overseas, with US orders just opening. To learn more, head on over to its website.

‘We Have to Take Over’: Far-Right Extremists Want to Seize Control of the GOP

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On Election Day last week, white nationalist Nick Fuentes urged his supporters on X to “get out and vote Democrat today.” The post racked up over 12 million views. Hours later, in the wake of the Democratic sweep, Fuentes took credit for the victory. “We did it again” he wrote on Telegram, later calling the phenomenon “the Groyper Effect,” a reference to the nickname given to his followers.

In recent days, Fuentes has claimed the election results are an opportunity for him and his allies on the far-right.

“Let the Democrats rule, see how that goes,” Fuentes said during a livestream last week. “There will come a time when the far left will terrorize people, and they're going to push all of them into the arms of the right. Who's going to fight the far left? Only the far-right. This is why we have to take over the right wing now.”

In another livestream last week, Fuentes added a warning to the GOP leadership: “It’s our party now … You need to cooperate with us… Groypers are open for business. It has [to] be America First—and if it's not, we'll do it again for the enemy.”

A WIRED review of comments from white supremacist, Christian nationalist, and other far-right groups in the wake of the midterm results shows much of the same. While the groups are uniformly attacking Trump’s failure to prioritize white, Christian American values, they also believe that this sudden shift to the left will result in a backlash that will see more people embrace far-right groups and ideologies.

“Many prominent figures in racist extremist movements have taken to declaring the MAGA movement dead since midterm elections, claiming that the results validate their previous criticisms of the Trump administration and opens new opportunities for their movements to push disaffected voters toward their ideological beliefs,” Jared Holt, senior researcher at Open Measures, tells WIRED.

While some of the Republican party’s extremist candidates were rejected during the elections last week, many did win their elections.

The Institute for Research and Education on Human Rights tracked 535 candidates running in statewide races who were members of at least one far-right Facebook group. The researchers found that of the 476 races that had been called by last Friday, 338 of those candidates were victorious, representing a 71 percent win rate (the vast majority of these races saw GOP incumbents retain their seats). In Florida, for example, 34-year-old citrus farmer Marshall Rawson, who attended the Unite the Right rally in Charlottesville in 2017, easily defeated his Democrat opponent in the state’s 10th District State House race.

Many states also passed ballot measures that could further far-right agendas, including anti-transgender rules, onerous voter ID laws, and anti-immigrant measures.

“Arizona, Colorado, Nebraska, and Washington approved bans on trans athletes in female sports, so girls in Washington State, for instance, will now have to go through a genital exam or pass a DNA test before they can participate in youth sports,” Devin Burghart, executive director with IREHR, said on Friday during a briefing with reporters.

The success of these ballot measures were celebrated by extremists groups as evidence that Americans wanted that the current GOP could not deliver.

The Proud Boys, the street-fighting group that Immigration and Customs Enforcement agency operations made largely redundant in recent years, were optimistic following the losses. “The ultimate rebuke of the current Republican establishment was citizens voting them out of office then doing their job for them by passing ballot measures on issues the Republicans should have already handled,” the Cape Fear Proud Boys wrote in their Telegram channel.

“What this signals to me is even in those places where they lost, they're getting 41-47 percent of the vote [so] there's still a mass base to try to be mobilized,” Chuck Tanner, research director at IREHR, said during the briefing. “That's the lesson that some of these groups are taking out of it, is that there's still that mass base.”

And it is the existence of this base, extremist groups claim, leaves the Republican Party vulnerable to takeover from the inside.

“Now is the time, if you're a nationalist, to start looking at moving on the Republican Party where you live,” Warren Balogh, a white supremacist from Pittsburgh who attended the Unite the Right rally in Charlottesville in 2017, wrote on his Telegram channel. “And by that I mean, organizing your guys and going in to do a hostile takeover.”

“This may be the best thing that has happened to the Republican party in a half-century,” the Cape Fear Proud Boys declared in a post on Telegram days after the election. “Now you will learn to placate us, or you will continue to fail. Understand this: You’re going to run people we like and you’re going to do what we say.”

But other extremists appear less inclined to wait until the next general election in 2028, preferring a more direct takeover.

"I'm not saying that we will win through democracy, I'm just saying we'll win,” Joel Webbon, a Christian nationalist pastor said last week in a video posted online after the election. Webbon and fellow far-right pastors, as well as guest speaker and noted conspiracy theorist Alex Jones, are set to meet at a conference in Texas this week where attendees will discuss, in at least one talk, “battle plans” for “Christian nationalists on the right to be able to seize power.”

Experts WIRED spoke to said they have not yet seen any explicit calls for violence from the far-right groups they are tracking, but some figures are already looking ahead of January 3, when Congress is set to certify the midterm election results.

Far-right activist and pardoned January 6 rioter Jake Lang, however, is planning an event in DC on January 3. It is unclear how many people are planning to attend, but one of the Christian nationalist pastors listed by Lang as an official speaker has already said he won’t be speaking.

Lang, who spent last weekend disrupting a cat convention that he thought was a furry convention, did not respond to a request for comment.
