Indiana Jones Star John Rhys-Davies Slams The Dial Of Destiny

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

James Mangold's 2023 adventure film "Indiana Jones and the Dial of Destiny" was met with a lot of ambivalence back when it was first released, and star John Rhys-Davies, who has played the role of Sallah in two of the previous movies, wasn't fond of the way it turned out either. In a video interview posted on the Off/Frame Instagram account, Rhys-Davies admitted to being very disappointed in the film, largely because it focused too much on all-new characters, rather than on Indy (Harrison Ford), and himself:

"I'll be honest with you, I was very disappointed in it. The film is meant to be a nostalgia film. They filled the cast up with people we had never met before, so there can be no nostalgia, really. I found the scenes that I'd been promised in the thing were cut immediately after I signed the contract. I think it's a disappointing film."

"Dial of Destiny," despite catching up with Indiana Jones in the year 1969, and featuring Sallah in a small role, was more focused on the adventures of the irascible Helena Shaw (Phoebe Waller-Bridge), Indy's adult goddaughter and something of a con woman who sells artifacts on the black market. Helena is a great character, I'd say, but Rhys-Davies has a point in that the movie was a little too focused on her, and not on the nostalgic characters the audience paid money to see.

And as an actor, of course, he has every right to be upset about his part being reduced after he signed his contract to appear in the movie. Rhys-Davies doesn't go into detail about what larger role Sallah was to play in "Dial of Destiny," but in the finished film he certainly doesn't play a major role at all.

John Rhys-Davies also noted that Antonio Banderas appears in "Indiana Jones and the Dial of Destiny," and that he wasn't utilized correctly either. Banderas plays a frogman named Renaldo whose screentime is very brief indeed. Regarding Banderas, Rhys-Davies said:

"And what is tragic, really, is ... I mean, there are a number of tragic things. I mean, Banderas, the most exciting actor of his generation. [...] I think he's a wonderful actor. But, really, what a terrible thing to do to him.

Some might defend the tone and the visuals of "Dial of Destiny," which are largely accurate to Steven Spielberg's Indy movies, but no one could argue that Antonio Banderas or John Rhys-Davies were used well. Perhaps the filmmakers didn't want to write a 2023 adventure story with two actors — Ford and Rhys-Davies — who were born in the 1940s.

It certainly could have been cheaper. "Dial of Destiny" was, according to some reports, a $419 million movie, making it possibly one of the most expensive movies of all time. This means that its worldwide box office total of just under $384 million didn't come close to turning a profit. /Film even saw the writing on the wall a week before the movie's release: "Dial of Destiny" wasn't going to make nearly enough.

Now, had the filmmakers scaled back on the film's action mayhem and caught up with an elderly Indiana Jones and Sallah who spent their time in libraries and slowly trekking into quiet caves, maybe you could have had something. Perhaps John Rhys-Davies could have played a much more central role. But then, an action-light Indiana Jones movie might be anathema to the nature of the character.

It has only been a few years since the release of "Indiana Jones and the Dial of Destiny," and the film doesn't seem to have undergone any kind of major relitigation in that time. The weary ambivalence towards the legacy sequel still seems to be firmly in place.

Reviews of "Dial of Destiny" were generally good, but no critic seemed to be all that overwhelmed. /Film's review, by Lex Briscuso, was positive, giving the film an eight out of 10, and said that "the film has a fun plot that feels completely true to the sense of adventure the series has always brought to its audience."

Although Owen Gleiberman's review in Variety just as accurately called it "a dutifully eager but ultimately rather joyless piece of nostalgic hokum." "Dial of Destiny" was the first Indiana Jones movie not to be directed by Steven Spielberg, and the entire affair felt like a tired victory lap with many new figures juggling the baton. It didn't help that star Harrison Ford was almost 81 when the movie was released to the public. He was hardly the same action star he was in "Raiders of the Lost Ark," released 42 years prior, and wasn't as well-suited for the fighting and whip-swinging the character was better known for.

"Dial of Destiny" isn't a low-quality embarrassment, but it's also simultaneously too large and too small. It only ever felt like a means to milk more money out of 1980s I.P., and never a serious step in the evolution of the character. John Rhys-Davies wasn't the only one who was disappointed.

French Judo Legend Teddy Riner Announces 2027 Presidential Bid, Drawing Praise From Emmanuel Macron

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

French judo superstar Teddy Riner, a five-time Olympic gold medalist and multiple world champion, has announced that he is running for president of France in 2027 in a fiery speech posted on social media.

The athlete, who has never held political office but is one of France’s most recognizable and popular sporting figures, portrayed himself as a political outsider determined to stand for ordinary French citizens which he feels have been let down. Related Stories XYZ Films, CAA Media Finance Ink U.S. Deal With Samuel Goldwyn Films for Dark Comedy 'Kill Me' Starring Charlie Day and Allison Williams (EXCLUSIVE)

Riner’s announcement comes at a particularly turbulent moment in France, just five months before a presidential election that has exposed deep divisions across the political spectrum.

The far-right party Rassemblement National is dominating the polls by a wide margin, with Marine Le Pen drawing between 33% and 37% of first-round voting intentions in recent surveys, roughly twice the support of her closest rivals. Jean-Luc Mélenchon, who leads the radical-left party La France Insoumise, has been gaining ground and is polling at around 17% to 19%, while the political center remains fragmented, with no candidate emerging to succeed Emmanuel Macron. The country has also been rocked by weeks of mass protests over the soaring cost of living and conditions in schools which escalated into violent riots.

“My name is Teddy Riner, and today I’m telling you straight to your face: I am a candidate in the 2027 presidential election,” he said while facing the camera and sitting at a desk in a presidential-style setting while bookshelves and a tricolor French flag in the background.

La résignation n'est pas une option. Face aux difficultés de notre pays, l'heure est venue de prendre ses responsabilités.⁰⁰Je suis candidat à l'élection présidentielle de 2027. ⁰⁰#TeddyRiner2027 #Présidentielle2027 #LaFrancequonveut pic.twitter.com/nM7rTfyTrH

“I know what people are going to say about me: ‘He’s crazy,’ ‘He’s not part of the establishment,’ ‘He doesn’t know the rules,’ ‘He’s never been involved in politics.’ But ask yourselves this question: What is really crazy today?”

Riner went on to paint a bleak picture of France, citing growing public debt, economic hardship and a breakdown of law and order.

“Is it crazy to refuse to watch France collapse under the weight of its debt, a little more every day? To see a country so rich pretend that it is poor?” he asked.

Riner criticized what he described as an unfair system that places an excessive burden on working people.

“Is it crazy to reject the hypocrisy of a system where those who get up early, work hard and follow the rules have no choice but to pay for everyone else?”

Macron reacted warmly to Riner’s announcement in an interview with the French broadcaster France 5 welcoming his decision to enter politics and praising his sense of duty. “Teddy Riner is a tremendous athlete who makes our country proud,” Macron said. “I supported him in competitions and as an athlete, and he is a committed man.”

Macron, who cannot seek a third consecutive presidential term in 2027, applauded the prospect of citizens from outside the political establishment taking part in the national debate.

“Political life, the life of the nation, does not belong to professionals,” Macron said, alluding to his own path, as he came into politics a decade ago after working in investment banking at Rothschild. “The life of the nation belongs to women and men who want to give their time, who are ready to commit themselves and fight difficult battles to make our country better. I am very happy that someone like Teddy Riner, who is a great champion, has decided to take part in this presidential debate and bring his convictions, his ideas and his talent,“ Macron continued.

Why Do I Go Numb Instead of Feeling Things?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 9, 2026 | Reviewed by Davia Sills

I was an actor in New York. Yes, that’s right—I didn’t emerge from the womb asking people how they felt for a living. I had dreams of being on Broadway, and after years of work, I finally got invited to the final callbacks for the lead role in a Broadway musical. For this final audition, I was going to wear my grandfather’s vintage pinstriped suit. I planned on changing into it at the audition studio to make sure it looked crisp. But while walking down 43rd Street, half a block from the audition, the suit fell out of my hands and into a pile of Central Park carriage horse manure. Terrified that I’d smell up the room and ruin my chances at the role, I rushed to the bathroom and tried to scrub out the stains before putting the suit on and walking into the audition.

Suffice it to say, I didn’t get the part. I got some generic feedback about how I didn’t have the right look. But I don’t really remember much of that part—the days and weeks afterward are a blank spot in my memory. And while I can recall the moment I dropped that suit on 43rd Street like it was yesterday, I can’t remember how I felt about not landing my big Broadway break. I can barely remember finding out I didn’t get it. The whole experience is a fog, as if the details and the feelings attached to them have been swallowed up together. When I reach back and try to remember how I felt about losing out on this role, the only thing I feel is... nothing.

Emotional numbing is a sense of being disconnected from your feelings. You might have a sense that something matters to you but not be able to feel much about it. Sometimes we disconnect like this in response to stress that threatens to overwhelm us. If we feel less, we can keep moving forward.

I see this often in work with clients—when moving towards a memory that holds a lot of charge, they’ll often just go blank, fuzz out, or suddenly be unable to recall details. I think of it like the villain from that ’80s movie, The NeverEnding Story: the Nothing. If you’re not an ’80s kid like me, the Nothing threatens to consume the magical land of Fantasia. It grows as people lose their hopes and forget their dreams. That’s the image that comes up for me when I try to describe my experience of it. I start to feel the big feeling coming on. Sometimes I even experience it for a moment. But as it threatens to overwhelm me, the Nothing swoops in so that I can move on.

While I invented my own ’80s fanboy name for what I’m experiencing, researchers have been exploring this topic for some time. There’s a related experience called alexithymia, which is difficulty identifying and describing our own emotions. Imagine being asked how you feel about a certain topic, reaching into wherever emotions live in us, and just hitting a wall. From the inside, it can be really hard to distinguish between “I don’t know how I feel” and “I don’t feel anything.”

Emotional numbness isn’t unique to men, but we do seem to have a corner on the market. And that makes sense given the messages we receive about what is and isn’t acceptable to feel. Psychologist Ronald Levant uses the term normative male alexithymia to describe a pattern found in many men who experience difficulty identifying and expressing emotion—particularly those emotions tied to vulnerability or intimacy. He believes this pattern is rooted in the way men are socialized in our society.

Those of us raised as men learn these messages early and often. We inherit them and gift them to each other: “Don’t cry. Don’t be so sensitive. Don’t let them see you care.” I honestly can’t remember where I first received this message. I’m tempted to chalk that one up to the Nothing, too.

But my Nothing isn’t a villain from a kids movie. I think of him as a part of me that learned to perform a job. And he performs it very well. I suspect the Nothing is the reason I’ve been able to succeed at grad school, to switch careers, to build a private practice. But the Nothing doesn’t discriminate between the pain of the story and the importance of the story itself: A friendship ends, and we convince ourselves it wasn’t that important anyway. A dream falls to manure (literally), and we find something else to want.

In my experience, the Nothing doesn’t just take away the disappointment. It also makes it harder to remember how much we cared. I wish that I could have given myself the time to really feel the disappointment and the sadness of seeing my dream slip through my fingers. My experience feels incomplete in my mind, like a book without the final chapter.

Sometimes in the middle of a session, a client goes blank. It’s not like they fully check out of the room, but they’ll be dipping their toe into an emotion and then just lose connection with it. When that happens, I’ll often invite them to notice what happens as they get closer to it. And sometimes they can’t. Sometimes the details are fuzzy, or there’s simply nothing there, and they get frustrated. That’s okay.

Noticing we can’t connect is a good place to start. Sometimes that blankness is doing a job, too. Rather than forcing our way through it, we can get curious about what feeling it might be protecting. And maybe, just maybe, that curiosity gives way to a name: sorrow, regret, grief. The point isn’t to choose the emotion we think we’re supposed to have. It’s to explore if we can connect with even the faintest sense of “Yes, that’s it.”

I think about younger me walking down 43rd Street with my grandfather’s suit, so excited and scared about what might happen next. I wish I could go back and tell him: “I see how much this matters to you. Whether you get the role or not, I want you to remember how much this matters. Don’t let that be taken from you.” We don’t have to convince ourselves a dream didn’t matter just because it didn’t come true. Sometimes, nearly 20 years later, we can give ourselves the chance to write about it and remember that it mattered.

Levant, R. F., & Parent, M. C. (2019). The development and evaluation of a brief form of the Normative Male Alexithymia Scale (NMAS-BF). Journal of Counseling Psychology, 66(2), 224–233. doi:10.1037/cou0000312

Schroders Emerging-Market Bond Strategy Moves Onto Solana Through Centrifuge

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Schroders is bringing an emerging-market bond strategy into the tokenized fund market through Centrifuge on Solana. The November 4 announcement concerns the Schroder International Selection Fund Emerging Market Bond strategy, with Centrifuge providing onchain infrastructure.

Schroders is bringing an emerging-market bond strategy into the tokenized fund market through Centrifuge on Solana. The November 4 announcement concerns the Schroder International Selection Fund Emerging Market Bond strategy, with Centrifuge providing onchain infrastructure. It is notable because the assets are not a cryptocurrency basket: investors are being offered a blockchain-native route to a conventional, actively managed fixed-income strategy with its own risks and investment processes.

Centrifuge says the initiative will introduce tokenized exposure to the Schroders emerging-market bond fund. Schroders manages more than $1 trillion in assets across its business, according to the announcement, and has decades of experience investing in debt issued by emerging-market governments and companies. That corporate scale provides context for the partnership; it is not an estimate of how much capital has entered this particular onchain fund. The launch represents Schroders' first Solana deployment, a specific milestone that should not be confused with a wholesale shift of the manager's portfolios to public blockchains.

Emerging-market bonds can offer access to currencies, credit risks and interest-rate conditions very different from those facing US Treasury bills. The underlying portfolio may be affected by sovereign finances, corporate defaults, currency movements and changes in global borrowing costs. Tokenization does not eliminate those exposures. It changes how interests in an investment strategy can be issued, recorded, distributed or serviced. Investors need to understand the fund's legal structure, eligibility, fees, dealing arrangements and the precise way token holdings map to recognized ownership. Those details ultimately determine whether the product meets their requirements.

Solana's appeal to financial product developers includes its transaction throughput and low per-transaction fees, while Centrifuge focuses on the specialist infrastructure needed to operate tokenized funds. The challenge is making asset management routines work with a public network without losing compliance controls. Fund subscriptions, redemptions, transfer restrictions and investor records must align with the legally constituted vehicle. A fast chain is helpful, but it cannot replace the fund administrator, custodian or manager. Operational success depends on the quality of those connections, not simply on the number of transactions the blockchain can process.

Much of the early RWA market concentrated on tokenized cash and short-dated US government debt. An actively managed emerging-market bond strategy is a different proposition because valuation, liquidity and portfolio turnover can be more complicated. A market may be open at all hours even when underlying bonds trade within narrower windows. That difference makes pricing and redemption policies essential. The value proposition could be broader distribution and improved processing, but investors should not infer that every share can be redeemed instantly or that tokenization creates round-the-clock liquidity in the underlying securities.

The next useful evidence would include an initial live issuance, assets held in the tokenized structure, participating distributors, supported jurisdictions and how subscriptions and redemptions actually settle. Over time, the question is whether blockchain infrastructure improves operational efficiency and attracts new investors without weakening fund protections. The announcement is a meaningful institutional experiment, but the practical success of the fund will rest on ordinary asset-management disciplines as much as on Solana technology.

Primary source: Centrifuge official product announcement. For additional context, see NewsBTC’s report on Solana’s infrastructure and institutional RWA growth.

Trump says Jensen Huang told him Monday he didn't know paramour 'was a spy'

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump said he spoke to Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang on Monday, and that Huang denied knowing that the woman he admitted having an extramarital affair with "was a spy" for China.

Trump told reporters at the White House that he was "amazed" by the allegations against Huang, which led Nvidia's board to initiate an independent investigation.

Trump suggested he had only recently learned of allegations that Megaspeed International executive Alice Huang was a Chinese spy.

"I didn't hear about it then, but a lot of people have heard about it, and Jensen totally denies that," Trump said.

"I asked him today because I did speak to him today just to see if he's feeling all right. 'How you doing?'" Trump said.

"And I asked him about that, and he ... said he had absolutely no idea that the person in question was a spy," the president said. "I asked him that question: 'Did you have any idea that she was a spy?' He said, 'I had no idea that she was a spy.' "

The New York Times on Oct. 29 reported that Alice Huang had been eyed by U.S. spy agencies "for possibly steering advanced Nvidia A.I. chips to China," and that "American officials had concluded she was a Chinese intelligence asset, funneling information to Beijing."

The Times reported that about a year ago, Trump was briefed about the intelligence findings "and chose not to do anything with the information." The newspaper noted that Trump has been reluctant to disrupt chip sales by Nvdia, which are boosting the U.S. stock market.

On Friday, The Times reported that a memo that circulated last spring among top Trump administration policymakers "cited intelligence that a Chinese businesswoman who had a close relationship with Nvidia's chief executive was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security."

The Times said the memo reported that Alice Huang was recruited to collect intelligence on the Nvidia CEO "as part of a drive by Beijing's top spy agency to learn more about American A.I. chips and potentially influence the company to push for its most advanced chips to be made available to China."

"I've been reading a little bit about it over a period of time, but not too long ago," Trump said Monday. "We'll look into it. I know the FBI is looking into it in terms of something else, and I think that he'll be in good shape."

Trump on Monday said of Jensen Huang, "He's suffering greatly."

"I believe he's suffering greatly. I read his statement, and I believe he's suffering greatly, more so because of his family, his wife, who he loves, and his family. But he is definitely suffering."

Asked if he would bring up the issue involving Huang with Chinese President Xi Jinping when the two leaders meet next week at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen, China, Trump said, "I don't think so."

"I mean, maybe I'm sure President Xi will ask me about it," Trump said. "I just know Jensen very well. I just think he's a very honorable, honorable man. I mean ... he built a great company, American company, and I think he's a very honorable guy, and I feel very confident that he'll be in good shape."

Trump's comments came as a Los Angeles law firm filed a class-action shareholder lawsuit against Nvidia and Huang in federal court in San Jose, California.

The Rosen Law Firm's suit alleges the defendants made false or misleading statements about his affair with Huang and Nvidia's relationship with Megaspeed, which presented a material risk to the company's reputation and business prospects.

Ford Just Proved Its EV Battery Health Barely Degrades After 5 Years

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

2021 Mustang Mach-E electric SUVs have kept most of their battery and range after thousands of miles on the road.

Real-world battery life for phones, laptops and other electronic devices can diminish over years of use, but does the same happen to electric cars after thousands of miles and hundreds of recharges? According to data published by Ford this week: Yes, but not as badly as you might fear.

The automaker published a report analyzing the battery state of health of more than 36,000 Mustang Mach-E electric SUVs sold to customers across the US and Canada during the initial 2021 model year. After more than five years on the road — and an estimated 2 billion cumulative miles driven — the fleet averaged 92% battery health.

I call that impressive, but not totally surprising. That battery durability data is largely in line with what I’ve seen reported across the industry by other automakers and independent analysts.

One of the biggest concerns of early EV adopters a decade ago (also shared by fence-sitters today) is long-term reliability. The battery is by far the most expensive component of an electric car, and owners want to know their shiny new EV will last as long as the combustion car it replaces.

Battery longevity is particularly important as more used EVs enter the market. Many experts (myself included) have touted pre-owned and off-lease EV deals as a way to bypass the electric-car price premium, but shoppers may be hesitant to roll the dice on higher-mileage vehicles. Many fear a used EV won’t meet their range needs or, worse, could blindside them with an expensive battery replacement.

Ford’s study is another data point that could help allay those worries.

Ford examined more than 36,000 individual vehicles delivered to customers across all 50 US states and Canada during the EV’s first year of sales. A little more than five years later, the automaker says that around 95% of vehicles surveyed retained between 87% and 97% of their battery health (an average of 92%) across the entire 2021 fleet after an estimated 2 billion cumulative miles driven.

Better battery health means an EV keeps delivering more of its advertised range as the odometer climbs. The 2021 Mustang Mach-E rolled off the assembly line with an EPA-estimated range between 211 and 305 miles, depending on the spec, which means that owners lost around 16 to 24 miles of range over time, according to my back-of-the-napkin math.

That said, there’s no beating the chemistry. Around 2,000 vehicles in the group crossed the 100,000-mile mark, retaining around 91% health. A smaller subset of about 30 drivers reached 200,000 miles in their Mach-Es, with an average battery health of 88%. More miles, it seems, means more wear.

At the extreme anecdotal end of the spectrum, one owner put more than 400,000 miles on his original battery — though Ford didn’t state the specific health of that 2022 Mach-E.

An independent study published by Aviloo earlier this year adds credence to Ford’s reported numbers. An Austrian EV battery diagnostics firm analyzed over 500,000 EVs from 20 global brands to measure battery degradation — at 50,000, 100,000 and 150,000 kilometers — finding that many popular models maintained over 90% battery health at high mileage.

Tesla’s Model Y, which competes directly with the Mach-E, was found to hold around 90.3% battery health at 150,000 kilometers (93,205 miles). Aviloo reported that the Volkswagen ID4 and Hyundai Ioniq 5 showed 90.6% and 92.8% retention, respectively, over the same surveyed mileage. The first-generation Nissan Leaf — a much older EV with a smaller battery — showed more degradation at 86.3%, an unfortunate double whammy given its shorter initial range.

According to Ford’s engineers and battery chemists, the Mach-E’s battery was designed to last at least 10 years, adding that the 2021 models in the study use an older nickel-cobalt-manganese, or NCM, chemistry.

Ford’s new Universal Electric Vehicle platform, which will underpin the upcoming Fathom small electric pickup, will use a newer lithium iron phosphate chemistry. That will come with unique charging needs and different battery management calibration than its Class of 2021. The UEV architecture is also built around robust bidirectional charging, so we’ll have to check back in five or so years to see how using your car to power your home affects long-term battery health.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/trump-administration-announces-10b-awards-rural-health-transformation-programs-second" hreflang="en">Trump administration announces $10B in awards for Rural Health Transformation Program’s second year</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Trump administration announces $10B in awards for Rural Health Transformation Program’s second year" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Trump administration announced Monday funding for the second year of the Rural Health Transformation Program (RHTP), with $10 billion in awards slated for all 50 states.

The U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) announced awards through the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services.

“President Trump made a historic investment in rural America, and HHS is putting those resources to work in every state,” said Robert F. Kennedy, Jr., HHS secretary, in a statement. “This next $10 billion will expand access to care, strengthen the rural health workforce, improve prevention, and help states build solutions that fit the needs of their own communities. We are giving rural America the resources and flexibility to build stronger health systems that deliver better care and better outcomes.”

Under the statute, half of the $10 billion-per-year distribution is split between the states evenly. The remaining half is determined by the CMS, with a portion of second-year funding being determined by states’ progress in implementing approved RHTP plans.

Federal officials say the annual review “strengthens accountability” for the investment.

Every state submitted applications to the federal government last November outlining plans to improve rural healthcare through their share of $50 billion in funds from the program, with CMS announcing first year awards in December.

Similar to fiscal year 2026, Texas and Alaska will receive the largest amount of funding as part of the program. Texas is set to receive $281,976,026, while Alaska will receive $272,653,149.

Other top recipients were Montana ($239,740,669), California ($231,105,823), Oklahoma ($227,097,543), Kansas ($225,896,117) and Georgia ($221,731,766).

New Jersey will receive the lowest amount of funding at $142,338,224, followed by Connecticut ($153,652,078), Rhode Island ($158,171,967), Massachusetts ($158,392,692) and Delaware ($160,294,521).

“Rural Americans have life expectancies three years shorter than their urban peers, but President Trump’s Rural Health Transformation Program is funding the historic investments necessary to close that gap,” said Mehmet Oz, M.D., CMS administrator, in a statement. “States have already put these resources to work bringing better care closer to home for their rural communities, and CMS looks forward to helping them build on that progress in the coming year.”

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/alignment-healthcare-sues-cms-over-its-2027-medicare-advantage-star-ratings" hreflang="en">Elevance Health sues over 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Elevance Health sues over 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Elevance Health is the latest insurer to sue the feds over its performance in the latest round of star ratings for the Medicare Advantage program.

The company sued the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services in District of Columbia federal court on Friday, arguing that the 2027 star ratings calculations violated the Administrative Procedure Act in using 11 measures that it says were adopted unlawfully.

In the lawsuit, the company said that the harm from including these measures is "concrete and substantial." If they were excluded, it would increase the scores for 12 of its Medicare Advantage contracts and generate $516 million in additional quality bonus payments.

"Their inclusion therefore does more than violate statutory requirements in the abstract: it depresses Elevance Health’s ratings and reduces the funding available for the following payment year," per the lawsuit. "That funding could support broader supplemental benefits, lower premiums and cost-sharing, and improved care coordination."

"CMS’s unlawful methodology puts those resources out of reach," the company said.

In addition to the financial impacts, Elevance argues that the star ratings scores can also lead to reputational damage that makes it harder for them to compete in the MA market. It also provides prospective enrollees with inaccurate information, the company said.

"Beneficiaries cannot make informed coverage choices when the government’s own comparison tool conveys a distorted assessment of the plans available to them," Elevance said.

"An artificially depressed rating does not merely place Elevance Health at a competitive disadvantage; it undermines the very comparison the program is supposed to enable," the company continued. "A rating lowered by unlawful measures presents beneficiaries with a misleading signal of relative quality, rather than a lawful basis for distinguishing among competing plans."

CVS Heath's Aetna is joining the ranks of insurers suing over its results in the 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

The insured filed suit Monday in Georgia's Southern District Court, and argues that 28 measures used by the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services to calculate its scores were "unlawfully adopted" and should have been excluded.

The court is the same venue that ruled in favor of Clover Health over the summer, and set aside 20 measures that were used to determine its 2026 scores. That decision, which the feds have appealed, kicked off a broad recalculation of 2026 scores that looked different for insurers outside of Clover, triggering a wave of litigation.

In its lawsuit, Aetna cites the Clover decision in finding that CMS extended beyond its statutory authority in deploying those 20 measures.

The company said that calculating its scores using the 28 measures in question will likely cost it $2 billion in 2028 quality bonus payments and rebate retention. Those are funds that the insurer could "apply toward expanding member benefits, reducing premiums and investing in quality improvement."

Aetna adds that in addition to the monetary loss, having lower scores negatively impacts the company's reputation and its ability to attract potential members.

"The harm to CVS Health is not exclusively financial; lower ratings carry meaningful reputational harm, which can in turn impact membership," per the lawsuit. "By incorporating unlawful measures into the 2027 Star Ratings calculation, CMS cast CVS Health’s ratings in a poorer light in the eyes of Medicare Advantage consumers relative to peer MAOs, undermining CVS Health’s ability to compete for Medicare beneficiaries through enhanced benefits and the like."

Aetna did not respond to a request for comment at the time of publication.

Based on an analysis from Stephens, 69% of Aetna's MA members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 81%. That threshold is the key to securing quality bonus payments.

Aetna's complaint follows others focused on the 2027 scores from Centene and Alignment Healthcare (see those stories below).

Centene has become the latest insurer to sue the federal government over its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

In a lawsuit filed Thursday in Georgia's Southern District Court, the company argues that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services violated the Administrative Procedure Act in determining its latest scores. Centene says that CMS ran afoul of "core statutory safeguards" in deploying 17 measures.

The use of those measures ultimately caused "significant injury" to the company, according to the complaint. For 2027, 9% of Centene's members are enrolled in plans with at least four stars, which declined from 18% in the prior year.

"In implementing each of the individual Star measures that Centene challenges, CMS violated applicable law and thereby caused Centene to lose out on hundreds of millions of dollars in revenue that Centene could use to strengthen benefits, reduce premiums, and improve health outcomes," the company said in the lawsuit.

CMS argues that the court has already sided with a similar argument in its decision in favor of Clover Health earlier this year. In that case, which led to a broad recalculation in scores, the courts set aside 20 measures that were used in determining Clover's star ratings for 2026.

If the 17 measures in question had been excluded in calculating Centene's scores, the company estimates it would receive $448 million more in quality bonus payments for the 2028 plan year, funds it would deploy to expand benefits, lower premiums and enhance programs like care coordination.

In addition to the financial impact, a drop in star ratings also carries a reputational hit, Centene said.

"Beyond the financial impact to the company and its members, depressed ratings also inflict significant reputational damage and diminished membership," the company said in the complaint

Centene had not returned a request for comment at the time of publication.

Alignment Healthcare sued federal regulators, alleging that its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings were determined "in flagrant violation" of statute.

In the lawsuit filed Friday in District of Columbia federal court, the insurer says that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services "has completely departed from Congress’s vision of leveraging predictable, valid measures developed with input from providers, plans, and the public" in establishing the methodology for the star ratings.

The company argues that rather than establish "consistent, stable specifications" for metrics through the traditional rule-making process, the agency instead detailed key measures through a series of "technical notes." This violates the Administrative Procedure Act, per the lawsuit.

The program's star ratings are the critical determinant in quality bonus payments to insurers as well as a tool that aims to make it easier for enrollees and brokers to identify benefits with the highest quality.

"Consistent application of the statutory requirements governing Star Ratings is therefore essential not only to plan payments and competition but also to ensuring that beneficiaries can rely on the Star Ratings as meaningful and comparable measures of plan quality," Alignment said in the lawsuit.

The company adds that multiple recent court decisions have set aside CMS' star ratings methodology, notably a ruling over the summer in favor of Clover Health that tossed 20 metrics used in the 2026 calculations. A Georgia federal judge determined that 10 measures utilized exceeded CMS' authority, while 10 other measures were promulgated inappropriately.

CMS recalculated the scores across the board following the ruling, but only set aside the 10 measures that the courts determined were outside the bounds of its regulatory authority. The agency maintained that approach in the 2027 calculations, Alignment argues.

"Congress created the Star Ratings program to allow Medicare Advantage beneficiaries to make informed decisions about plan enrollment based on clinically validated measures of plan quality that had been subjected to notice and comment," the company said. "CMS’s contrary approach fails to carry out the vision that Congress set forth by statute."

The Trump administration has appealed the Clover decision, so that legal case remains ongoing.

In addition, Alignment argues it suffered reputational and financial damage as a result of its 2027 scores, which plummeted compared to the prior year. Just 25% of its members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 100% in 2026.

Shares in the company tumbled following the release of the ratings, though stock prices have rebounded since.

Alignment sued the feds following the Clover decision to secure a recalculation for its scores that aligns with the court's decision, and in a statement to Fierce Healthcare, said that the lawsuit aligns with that perspective.

"Our lawsuit seeks to ensure that the Star Ratings program operates as Congress intended and that Alignment is properly compensated for the care we provide," Alignment said in the statement. "We respect the judicial process and will present our case in court. Because this matter involves pending litigation, we will not comment on the specific legal arguments beyond our filings."

"Our focus remains on delivering exceptional care, improving health outcomes and advocating for a stable, lawful regulatory framework that supports seniors and strengthens the Medicare Advantage program," the company said.

Researchers Recover DNA from 7,000-Year-Old Leather, Revealing a Link to Goats Alive Today

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ancient DNA recovered from a remarkably preserved piece of 7,000-year-old leather is shedding new light on how prehistoric communities in Spain used domesticated animals.

A 7,000-year-old piece of goat leather discovered in a Spanish burial cave has yielded ancient DNA, allowing scientists to trace a genetic connection between prehistoric livestock and goats living in Spain today. The exceptionally preserved material also provides direct evidence that Early Neolithic communities were processing the hides of domesticated animals.

The findings, published November 4, 2026, in the journal PLOS One, come from a study led by Jolijn A. M. Erven of University College Dublin and colleagues. Their analysis offers a rare glimpse into animal husbandry and leatherworking on the Iberian Peninsula thousands of years before written records.

The leather fragment was recovered from Cueva de los Murciélagos, a burial site in southern Spain known for preserving ancient organic materials. Radiocarbon dating placed the specimen at approximately 7,000 years old. Marks on its surface indicate that the hide had been tanned and perforated, suggesting it was once part of a garment or protective covering before being buried alongside human remains.

Recovering DNA from such ancient leather is particularly difficult in warmer regions, where heat and environmental conditions accelerate the breakdown of genetic material. Despite extensive fragmentation, researchers extracted both mitochondrial DNA, which is inherited through the maternal line, and nuclear DNA, which contains genetic information from both parents.

Francisco Martínez-Sevilla emphasized the unusual preservation conditions: “The preservation of this 7,000-year-old goat leather is exceptional. Cueva de los Murciélagos has preserved organic materials that rarely survive in the archaeological record, offering us a unique opportunity to investigate prehistoric technologies, animal husbandry, and the relationships between human communities and domestic animals.”

Genetic analysis identified the leather as coming from a domestic goat rather than a wild Iberian ibex. Its DNA showed a close relationship to the modern Bermeya goat breed of northern Spain, indicating that a related genetic lineage has persisted on the Iberian Peninsula for thousands of years.

The researchers also detected no evidence of African genetic ancestry in the ancient specimen, unlike the ancestry found in modern European goats. One possible explanation is that genetic exchange involving African goat populations occurred after the Neolithic period. However, the incomplete DNA and limited sample size prevent firm conclusions about when these connections developed or what they might indicate about prehistoric interactions between Iberia and Africa.

Kevin Daly highlighted the genetic link between ancient and living animals: “It is rare to for such ancient, organic materials to retain DNA – but this was one of the few exceptions. With a tiny amount of surviving DNA, we were able to show a link with Iberian breeds today – a genetic connection spanning 7,000 years. This study demonstrates what can done through the collaboration of international multidisciplinary teams – transforming an archaeological object into a link with the living present.”

Reference: “Genetic analysis of 7,000 year old preserved goat leather from Cueva de los Murciélagos (Albuñol, Spain)” by Jolijn A. M. Erven, Zoé Robinet Guyet, Louis L’Hôte, Valeria Mattiangeli, Eva Fernández-Domínguez, Geordie Laidlaw, Rafael M. Martínez Sánchez, Blas Ramos Rodríguez, Maria Herrero-Otal, Pedro Henríquez Valido, José Antonio Lozano Rodríguez, The VarGoats consortium, Daniel G. Bradley, Kevin G. Daly and Francisco Martínez-Sevilla, 4 November 2026, PLOS ONE. DOI: 10.1371/journal.pone.0358468

Funding: This research has been carried out within the framework of different research projects: ‘De los museos al territorio: actualizando el estudio de la Cueva de los Murciélagos of Albuñol (Granada)’ (MUTERMUR) (CM/JIN/2021-009, to F. M.-S.) and ‘La Cueva de los Murciélagos of Albuñol una ventana única a la Prehistoria del sur de Europa’ (MUTERMUR 2.0) (CM/DEMG/2024-046, to F. M.-S.) financed by the Comunidad de Madrid and Universidad de Alcalá (directed by F.M.S.). DC’s Psychological Thriller Officially Removed From 532 Theaters

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

He has covered everything from Marvel to the Oscars, and Marvel at the Oscars. He also writes obsessively about the box office, charting the many hits and misses that are released weekly, and how their commercial performance shapes public perception. In his time at Collider, he has also helped drive diversity by writing stories about the multiple Indian film industries, with a goal of introducing audiences to a whole new world of cinema.

While it hasn't exactly performed like a regular superhero movie at the box office, the latest installment of the DC Universe is meeting the expectations of its genre. Whether this qualifies as a success for the brand remains to be seen, but for the DC Universe to experiment so early in its run is worth commending. Instead of building toward an Avengers-like mega-budget crossover event, the franchise has so far produced disparate films and shows across genres. The results have been hit or miss, with James Gunn's Superman reboot doing solid business but the Supergirl movie bombing terribly.

Following the disappointing performance of Supergirl this summer, all eyes were on the third feature installment of the franchise. The movie in question has spent two weeks in release, which means that it experienced its first drop in theater count this week. Because the movie wasn't front-loaded like most horror offerings, in addition to a positive reception from both critics and audiences, the decline has been rather controlled. For instance, Supergirl — directed by Craig Gillespie and starring Milly Alcock — was removed from 1,100 theaters in its third week.

Produced on a reported budget of more than $180 million, the film ended its global run with $125 million. It received mixed reviews and is now sitting at a 52% score on the aggregator website Rotten Tomatoes. Alcock will reportedly return as the character in next year's Man of Tomorrow, starring David Corenswet as Superman and Nicholas Hoult as Lex Luthor. Directed by Gunn, the third installment in the DCU's Superman saga will be released in July 2027.

Four villains, and four arguments the films were honest enough to actually let them make. Here is the one you lean towards, the part of it that holds up, and the exact point where it stops holding up.

You align with the one who was right about the problem and wrong about the solution. Killmonger is the most persuasive villain Marvel has produced because the film never pretends his anger came from nowhere.

You align with the protector, which is the most understandable and most dangerous position on this list. Namor is not chasing power or revenge, he is doing whatever keeps his people hidden, and he has been doing it for centuries.

You align with the patient one, the only man on this list with no powers, no army and no hurry. Zemo lost his family in Sokovia and then spent years working out that you do not fight the Avengers, you introduce them to each other's secrets.

You align with the one who decided the answer was arithmetic. Thanos is not angry and not grieving in any way he will admit to, he simply reached a conclusion and never once revisited it, which is precisely what makes him frightening.

The franchise's latest installment, of course, is Clayface. Designed as a comparatively lean anti-hero spin-off in the vein of Joker, the movie received positive reviews and is now sitting at a "Certified Fresh" 78% critics' score on Rotten Tomatoes. More impressively, the film's audience score on the aggregator is perched at a "Verified Hot" 94% — extremely rare for the horror genre. The site's consensus reads, "Carving out a unique identity for itself in the DCU while closely resembling a litany of body horror classics, Clayface takes time to get going until it eventually morphs into an icky and satisfying revenge thriller."

Having debuted in 3,478 theaters two weeks ago, Clayface lost 532 theaters this week. It's heading into its third week of release with $92 million in the bank globally against a reported budget of $44 million, playing in 3,946 theaters. Starring Tom Rhys Harries, Naomi Ackie, Max Minghella, and Eddie Marsan, the movie was directed by James Watkins.

Landstar: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Regulatory shifts and stricter vetting are reshaping Landstar's competitive moat as freight recovery gains momentum.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Landstar (LSTR -1.58%)reported total revenue of $1.512 billion, up 25% year-over-year. Freight capacity is tightening rapidly across truckload sectors as market conditions begin shifting in favor of transportation providers. A resilient balance sheet and a network of independent owner-operators position the company to capture upside during this freight recovery. Three things management said stand out.

Oversized equipment transport is expanding rapidly through demand in data centers, energy, and infrastructure. This specialized capability yields higher revenue per load than standard flatbed cargo.

Recent court rulings on broker liability are forcing independent freight brokers to reevaluate their exposure to litigation. Joining an established network gives independent brokers administrative backing and insurance protection.

Carrier vetting standards have significantly reduced third-party truck count over four years. Eliminating higher-risk carriers helps protect overall margins from severe liability claims and supply chain theft.

Strong capital discipline and fleet reinvestments reinforce the business model as shipping rates normalize.

7 Western Shows That Are Better Than 'Yellowstone'

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In June 2018, a neo-Western premiered on the Paramount Network that would revitalize the genre. That show was called Yellowstone, created by Taylor Sheridan and John Linson, snagging the iconic Kevin Costner to lead a talented, stellar cast. The series takes place in Montana and follows John Dutton (Costner) as he and his family, the overly ambitious Jamie (Wes Bentley) and the smart, loose cannon Beth (Kelly Reilly) try to defend their ranch from those trying to poach it away from them — from the Indian reservation that wants to transform the Yellowstone-Dutton Ranch back to its pristine, original form, to a global conglomerate that wants to transform the land into a modern paradise for tourists. While trying to fight off those who want their land, the Duttons have to contend with their own family issues.

We didn't know it at the time, but what we turned into on June 20, 2018, was a show that would bend the rules of the traditional Western. Yellowstone wasn't your typical, family-friendly Western show. This series was gritty, violent, and filled to the brim with drama and treachery at every turn. Yellowstone is, by far, one of the best neo-Westerns of all-time, one that you can binge-watch over-and-over without ever getting bored with it. While the show has etched its name in pop culture history, there are Western shows out there that, for all intents and purposes, are just as good, or better, than Yellowstone. So, if you're on your 50th viewing, and are now trying to search out for something better, here are the Westerns that are definitely better than Yellowstone.

In the 1950s, Western shows were just gaining popularity, and one show that many viewers tuned into week-after-week was Bonanza. The NBC show, which premiered in 1959, is one of the longest-running Western shows of all time, and watching just one episode of this legendary series, you'll quickly see why it had so much staying power.

Set in the 1860s, Bonanza follows the Cartwright family, a wealthy clan who lives in Virginia City, Nevada during the Gold Rush era. That little gold nugget of information is important, because the show is basically centered on mining, especially silver ore, which was under the town of Virginia City and was discovered in 1859. Bonanza has all the Western tropes that one would love, and its opening theme is iconic in TV history. Notable for being one of TV Guide's 50 Greatest Shows of All Time, Bonanza is certainly one of the best Westerns ever.

When you think about how the Western genre on television came to be, you have to trace its roots back to The Lone Ranger, which brought popularity to Western TV shows and was ABC's first bonafide ratings hit. Starting out as a popular radio series, The Lone Ranger made the jump to television in 1949, and saying it was an instant hit is a drastic understatement.

The Lone Ranger follows a group of Texas Rangers who get ambushed, and all are nearly killed, except one, who is known as "The Lone Ranger" (John Hart for 52 episodes, Clayton Moore for 169 episodes), and teams up with Tonto (Jay Silverheels), a Native American who saved and nursed The Lone Ranger back to health. Much like Bonanza, The Lone Ranger helped popularize Westerns on TV, which was just coming into the mainstream. The episodes are exciting, and the characters are iconic. Unlike Yellowstone, The Lone Ranger is more friendly for families, which makes it a better watch for TV viewers who want to introduce Westerns to the little ones.

Before Yellowstone became the vanguard of the neo-Western, you could make an argument that FX's Justified was the show that popularized the sub-genre. Based on the novel Fire in the Hole by Elmore Leonard, Justified is a unique throwback to the Westerns of old, but with a more modern twist that contemporary Western fans would come to expect, especially after watching Yellowstone.

The series follows Raylan Givens (Timothy Olyphant), a Deputy U.S. Marshal who does his work the way Marshals did in the 1800s. This way of investigating makes him a frequent target for criminals, especially Boyd Crowder (Walton Goggins), a career criminal known for robbing banks. Just by reading that, you get the sense of what Justified is. Having an old-school Western show set in the modern age was a bold move for the show creators and runners to make, but it was one that paid off thanks to the awesome acting and intriguing storylines. If it wasn't for Yellowstone, we would be talking about Justified being the vanguard of neo-Westerns, without question.

When you think of Westerns, most people think of gunfights in the middle of town, and law enforcement officers trying to hunt down criminal bank robbers. While most of the best Westerns have these tropes, not all in the genre has to be action-packed, gun-fighting bonanzas. Take Little House on the Prairie, for example, the famed NBC Western that went the historical drama route to its claim to fame.

Based on Laura Ingalls Wider's Little House on the Prairie novel, the series follows the Ingalls family, who move to Walnut Grove, Minnesota in the 1840s, and most of the show revolves around their lives, which is told through the lens of Charles (Michael Landon) and Caroline's (Karen Grassle) middle daughter, Laura (Melissa Gilbert), and her maturation from child to adulthood. Little House on the Prairie is well-known for its story of "strength through family," with the Ingalls and other Walnut Grove townsfolks finding a way to make it through the strength of family and community. This is completely separate from what Yellowstone represents, which saw family as more of a hindrance than anything else. If you're looking for a Western drama that is more family-focused, Little House on the Prairie is definitely your show.

The show that claimed the most of your answers is the world you were built for. If two tied, both are shown — you're complicated enough to straddle two Sheridan universes.

You are a Dutton — or you might as well be. You understand that some things are worth protecting at any cost, and that the modern world's indifference to history, to land, to legacy, is not something you're willing to accept quietly. You lead from the front, you carry your family's weight without complaint, and when someone threatens what's yours, you don't escalate — you finish it. You're not cruel. But you are absolute. In Yellowstone's world, that combination of ferocity and loyalty doesn't make you a villain. It makes you the only thing standing between everything that matters and everyone who wants to take it.

You thrive in the chaos of high-stakes negotiation, where the money is enormous, the margins are thin, and the wrong word in the wrong room can cost everyone everything. You're a fixer — the person called when a situation is already on fire and needs someone with the nerve to walk into it. West Texas oil country rewards exactly what you are: sharp, adaptable, unsentimental, and absolutely clear-eyed about what people want and what they'll do to get it. You're not naive enough to think this world is fair. You're smart enough to be the one deciding who it's fair to.

You are a Dwight Manfredi — someone who has served their time, paid their dues, and arrived somewhere unexpected with nothing but their reputation and their wits. You adapt without losing yourself. You build loyalty through respect rather than fear, though you're not above reminding people that the two aren't mutually exclusive. Tulsa King is for people who are still standing when everyone assumed they'd be finished — who find, in an unfamiliar place, that they're more capable than the world gave them credit for. You don't need a throne. You build one, wherever you happen to land.

You carry the weight of a system that is broken by design, and you do it anyway — because someone has to, and because you're the only one positioned to do it without the whole thing collapsing. Mike McLusky's world is for people who are comfortable operating where there are no good options, only less catastrophic ones. You speak every language: law enforcement, criminal, political, human. That fluency makes you invaluable and it makes you a target. You've made your peace with both. Mayor of Kingstown belongs to people who understand that keeping the peace is not the same as being at peace — and who do the job regardless.

By the late 1950s, Westerns were the staple on television, especially for ABC, which already had a massive Western hit on their hands with The Lone Ranger. However, with that series coming to an end in 1957, they needed another Western to fill its timeslot and continue its ratings' dominance. Enter Maverick, a Western comedy created by Roy Huggins which is just as iconic as the series it replaced.

More comedy-focused than The Lone Ranger, Maverick follows Bert Maverick (James Garner), a poker-playing rounder who is always on the lookout for games with high stakes. Most of the storylines within Maverick center on Maverick's high-stakes poker-playing, and this is where the comedy is born. Maverick wasn't always about poker, however. Make no mistake, this was a Western in the 1950s, so you know there were villains that were able gunslingers as well. Maverick is a truly exciting Western series, one that we hope today's Hollywood will not touch and remake. It's that timeless.

With Yellowstone being a massive hit, there was little doubt that the show was going to spawn a franchise. The first show to spin-off from its parent series was 1883, created by Taylor Sheridan, and, if one gives it a chance, would see that it actually exceeds its parent show in both story and tone.

1883 is a prequel that follows the Dutton family at the end of the Civil War. As they pack up and leave Tennessee, they begin to head west, eventually settling in Montana and putting up stakes in the land that would later become the massive Yellowstone-Dutton Ranch. Filmed as a miniseries, 1883 is a great look at how the Dutton family came to be and the struggles they endured to make a home and living for themselves in those tough times. By ditching the heightened drama and toxic vitriol of Yellowstone, 1883 is a more traditional Western drama that follows the rules, not aims to break them. So, for fans of old-school Westerns who were turned off by Yellowstone's breakneck drama, 1883 is a show that's more your speed.

We've listed some truly iconic Western shows that are certainly better than Yellowstone. However, we have yet to discuss the show that outclasses them all, especially Yellowstone, and that show is Gunsmoke. Starting out as a radio series, like other Westerns at the time, Gunsmoke made the jump to TV in 1955, thanks to CBS. No one at CBS, however, would have guessed that Gunsmoke would be a staple of the network for 20 years.

Set in Dodge City, Kansas after the Civil War, Gunsmoke follows U.S. Marshal Matt Dillion (James Arness), who protects the citizens of Dodge City from criminals. When one talks about traditional Westerns, Gunsmoke is the show that is often brought up as an example of how to do it right, and exciting. You'll get it all here, from drama to exhilarating gun-fights, all packed in a single episode. With its strong Western tone, and great plotlines, there is no question Gunsmoke is certainly better than Yellowstone.

10 Best The Godfather Parodies, Ranked (Including Modern Family)

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Francis Ford Coppola's The Godfather has provided movies with an abundance of parody opportunities. Released in 1972, the crime epic established a remarkably distinctive cinematic language, from Marlon Brando's gravelly delivery and intimidating presence to the elaborate rituals of loyalty, family gatherings, and underworld negotiations. Yet, The Godafther's influence extends well beyond gangster movies, with countless filmmakers borrowing its imagery, dialogue, performances, and instantly recognizable musical themes.

Part of what makes The Godfather such an effective target for comedy is the contrast between its characters' deadly seriousness and the situations surrounding them. Vito's quiet authority, Michael's increasingly elaborate schemes, and the formalities of organized crime are all wonderfully easy to exaggerate. Even relatively minor details, such as kissing a mob boss's hand or making somebody an offer they cannot refuse, can become hilarious when transplanted into an entirely different setting.

The Godfather’s most recognizable sequences provide an endless supply of opportunities for visual comedy. The wedding celebrations, infamous horse-head incident, and climactic baptism montage have all become familiar enough to audiences that a relatively straightforward recreation can deliver an immediate hilarious punchline.

Animaniacs has a knack for sending up popular movies within a short scene. Its recurring "Goodfeathers" segments, which follow a trio of pigeons modeled on characters from gangster cinema, introduce an especially memorable take on Vito Corleone with the Godpigeon.

An obese, 96-year-old pigeon, the Godpigeon is the boss of the Goodfeathers and makes his authority known by expecting the trio to kiss his foot whenever he appears. His raspy, largely unintelligible mumbling is a direct nod to Brando's performance in The Godfather, and he usually needs Bobby to translate what he's saying.

Despite being treated like an intimidating criminal patriarch, the Godpigeon is generally jovial and would much rather enjoy life as a pigeon than get caught up in the trio's problems. The Godpigeon also appears in several Goodfeathers segments in Animaniacs, so the joke isn't limited to one fleeting appearance.

South Park finds an appropriately petty reason to recreate one of The Godfather Part II's most chilling sequences. In "Toilet Paper," the boys toilet-paper their teacher's house, but Cartman becomes increasingly concerned that Kyle's guilt will eventually urge him to confess. His solution is to take Kyle out on a boat at Stark's Pond and kill him.

The setup deliberately echoes Michael Corleone's attempt to have his brother Fredo murdered during a fishing trip in The Godfather Part II. The accompanying music also references the original film's "Godfather Waltz," giving Cartman's ludicrous plan the sort of ominous atmosphere usually reserved for a major betrayal within a criminal empire.

Of course, Cartman isn't Michael Corleone. He's a vindictive child trying to cover up a school prank, and his murderous plan is undermined by the fact that he only has a Wiffle bat.

Angelica Pickles may be a toddler, but she already understands the value of having people owe her favors. In Rugrats in Paris: The Movie, she adopts the persona of the "Bobfather," complete with a tuxedo and her beloved doll Cynthia positioned in her lap. It's an unmistakable take on Vito Corleone's introduction in The Godfather.

Angelica's fellow babies come to her with requests, and she treats their problems like business propositions. The scene climaxes with Phil and Lil producing the broken head of a wooden rocking horse they found in their crib. They ask the Bobfather what happened, only for Angelica to explain that it's what they get for wiping their boogers on Cynthia.

It's a wonderfully silly adaptation of the infamous horse-head scene, transforming one of cinema's most disturbing threats into a punishment for a pair of toddlers with poor manners.

Jim Abrahams’ Mafia! is an entire comedy built around sending up gangster movies, with The Godfather and The Godfather Part II among its principal targets. It depicts the Cortino family's rise from Sicily to the criminal underworld of America, with the story moving between generations in a deliberately familiar gangster-movie structure.

Mafia!'s most direct Brando parody comes through Lloyd Bridges as the older Vincenzo Cortino. Bridges gets to send up the famous tomato-and-death scene, while the movie repeatedly turns familiar mafia conventions into opportunities for ridiculous sight gags.

Even the family's journey to America gets a joke, when Ellis Island officials attempt to rename the immigrant Vincenzo after his Armani windbreaker. It may not be as consistently sharp as Abrahams' Airplane!, but its affectionate mockery of gangster cinema gives it plenty of entertaining material.

One of the funniest uses of The Godfather in modern animation comes from Disney's Zootopia, which introduces Mr. Big, an Arctic shrew who happens to be one of the city's most intimidating crime bosses. The character recreates Brando's Corleone with a tiny body, a raspy whisper, a formal tuxedo, and an entourage of enormous polar bear enforcers.

This contrast is particularly funny, juxtaposing the gangster grandeur with the diminutive creature. Like The Godfather, Judy Hopps and Nick Wilde are brought before Mr. Big during his daughter Fru Fru's wedding reception.

The entire sequence deliberately recreates the atmosphere and staging of the original film's opening, with Judy and Nick discovering that their investigation has taken them into considerably more dangerous territory. The filmmakers even recreated elements of the original film's set and dialogue.

Homer Simpson's brief career as a Krusty the Clown impersonator takes an unexpected turn when he is mistaken for the real Krusty and becomes embroiled with the Springfield Mafia. In The Simpsons season 6, "Homie the Clown," Homer is captured by Fat Tony to repay Krusty’s debt.

The episode's climactic sequence features Homer attempting to entertain Fat Tony and his associates with a dangerous bicycle stunt. While Fat Tony and his associates are already a Godfather spoof, the episode’s best reference sees Homer hit his head on a series of hanging wine glasses that ring out Nino Rota's famous "Love Theme” from The Godfather.

The mob standoff is perfectly executed and prompts some genuine tension. Of course, The Simpsons quickly undermines this, with the reveal that Krusty’s debt is just $48 becoming the biggest joke.

The Muppets have tackled plenty of famous movies, and their Godfather spoof gives Kermit the Frog a suitably imposing new identity as the Frogfather. In this 1999 Movie Mania short, Fozzie Bear visits him to ask for revenge against Statler and Waldorf, whose constant heckling has finally gone too far.

The premise is already funny because Fozzie's request is essentially a complaint about two elderly audience members who won't stop telling jokes at his expense. Kermit plays the part with the serious demeanor of Brando's Vito Corleone, treating the matter as though it concerns a genuine threat to the family business.

Naturally, the Muppets can't resist turning the idea of a mob hit into something considerably sillier. The Frogfather dispatches his henchmen to carry out a classic, non-lethal execution – smashing cream pies straight into the faces of Statler and Waldorf to permanently silence their heckling

Before Fat Tony became one of The Simpsons’ most recognizable supporting characters, he made his debut in "Bart the Murderer." The season 3 episode sees Bart stumble into a job at a mafia social club after a series of unfortunate events leaves him working as the group's bartender.

The episode draws on both The Godfather and Goodfellas, with Bart becoming increasingly comfortable around the mobsters as they treat him like one of their own. He serves drinks, listens to their stories, and enjoys the perks of his new employment, even as the gang's criminal activities become harder to ignore.

Things take a darker turn when Principal Skinner disappears after Fat Tony and his crew threaten to deal with him. The parody draws much of its humor from dropping a schoolboy into the criminal organization and letting him enjoy the lifestyle before discovering the consequences.

Marlon Brando parodying his own most famous role is an irresistible premise, and Andrew Bergman's The Freshman makes excellent use of it. Brando plays Carmine Sabatini, a powerful New York importer whose resemblance to Vito Corleone is so striking that Matthew Broderick's film student, Clark Kellogg, is immediately taken aback when they meet.

Clark initially accepts a job with Carmine after being introduced by Victor, a small-time crook played by Bruno Kirby. His work soon becomes considerably stranger than he anticipated. One of his first assignments is collecting a Komodo dragon from the airport and delivering it to a secretive gourmet club that specializes in endangered animals.

Brando wholly leans into the mannerisms and screen presence that made Vito Corleone so recognizable. Carmine is imposing, sentimental, and capable of making even an ordinary conversation feel like a negotiation. It's a wonderfully silly extension of his most iconic role.

Modern Family's "Fulgencio" perfectly recreates The Godfather’s famous baptism montage. Like the original, it intercuts the religious ceremony with a series of acts of revenge.

As Phil renounces Satan and all his works during the baptism, his son Luke is busy settling various suburban grievances on his behalf. He releases rats into Karen Sullivan's party, causes Lee Cooper's camper to roll into a lemon tree, shoots balloons covering Phil's billboard, and gets school bully Reuben to admit that he has been lying – who wakes up to discover the head of a stuffed zebra in his bed.

The sequence also plays with the original Godfather's closing image when Phil closes a door on Claire after she starts asking questions. It’s so unexpected but so perfect that it stands as the greatest Godfather parody.

HBO’s Most Unconventional Series Is Officially Surging on Streaming

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Nathan Fielder is a genius, and that's no debate. He is one of the few creatives whose work has consistently received rave reviews thanks to his dedication to the craft. Part comedic and part dramatic, Fielder has found a way to take serious topics and infuse them with humor while doing serious reporting. It means something when his lowest-rated project on Rotten Tomatoes has a 68% score. However, one of his best shows is an HBO dramedy that is seeing a resurgence on streaming.

This show features Fielder's signature absurd humor, meant to lighten serious topics. Two seasons have aired so far, and neither has a score below 95%. Titled The Rehearsal, the show takes normal situations and tries to remove the pressure by rehearsing them. So, whether it's a job interview, a wedding, or even a date, the rehearsal is meant to remove the guesswork and make the event feel familiar, even if it's quite new. The show goes to great lengths to recreate situations to make them feel as real as possible. It hasn't been without its controversies, sometimes landing Fielder in hot water with the public or even companies like Paramount and the FAA.

Still, some of these qualities — like fake singing competition participants not knowing it's a show or satirizing Paramount by comparing it to the Nazi regime — are what make The Rehearsal feel fresh and entertaining. The series has seen serious streaming gains over the last two weeks, with FlixPatrol consistently ranking it in the top ten. This surge coincides with the release of Fielder's latest project, which has been receiving rave reviews from critics and fans.

A great detective is really just a personality with a method attached, which is why we keep remaking the same four of them. Here is yours, what it says about you, and the case for them being the best in the business.

You let people think you are not a threat and then you simply never go away. Columbo is the only major detective show that tells you who did it in the first ten minutes, because the pleasure was never the puzzle, it was watching a rich man slowly realise he is in trouble.

You are the most interesting person in the room and you know exactly how to use it. Rian Johnson built Blanc as a gentleman detective who is also an outsider everywhere he goes, which lets him be charming at people who would never otherwise talk to him.

Everybody tells you everything, and you have never once asked them to. Jessica Fletcher is a crime novelist from a small town in Maine who is so warm and so obviously trustworthy that suspects confess to her essentially as a social courtesy.

You expect to be lied to and you are almost never disappointed. Chandler wrote Marlowe as a man with a rigid private code in a city that has none, which is why he takes the case, solves it, gets nothing out of it and takes the next one.

At her peak, Elizabeth Holmes was a success like no other. She founded Theranos, a blood-testing startup that made her a billionaire, but her downfall was swift when it was revealed she deceived investors. She was sentenced to 11 years in prison, which was reduced to 10. She has been the subject of numerous projects, including Hulu's The Dropout and HBO's The Inventor: Out for Blood in Silicon Valley.

34 days before going to prison, Holmes invites a skeptical film crew to her home. That crew is Fielder's, and what emerges is a 3-hour masterpiece titled You Can See Everything with a 96% score from critics and viewers on Rotten Tomatoes. The A24 documentary has succeeded by most metrics, including a $65 million box office haul against a $7 million production budget. Reports say A24 is considering submitting the movie for a Best Picture nomination.

Stream The Rehearsal on HBO Max in the U.S. and stay tuned to Collider for more updates.

Trump says he was ‘only kidding’ about making Dana White press secretary

President Trump said he was "only kidding" when he suggested UFC CEO Dana White could be the press secretary, while adding that White "loves the idea" and is a "very capable guy."

President Donald Trump said Sunday he was joking when he announced on social media that he had offered UFC boss Dana White the White House press secretary job and lined up a roster of fighters to serve as his assistants.

"I will tell you, I was only kidding," Trump said when asked what prompted the pitch and whether he was serious.

Trump pointed to the fighters he had named as White’s would-be assistants, saying the lineup should have given the joke away. "And you see a lot of rough faces there," he said.

He also joked that some of the fighters came from "parts unknown."

President Donald Trump and Dana White, UFC president and CEO, arrive for UFC Freedom 250 on the South Lawn of the White House in Washington on June 14, 2026. (Alex Brandon/AP)

Trump said he thought White liked the idea and called him "a very capable guy." As for how the UFC boss might handle the job, Trump said, "You wouldn’t mess around with him so much."

"But Dana is actually coming tomorrow," Trump said. He explained that the visit was for other reasons, saying he believed White already planned to be in Washington and would stop by to see him.

UFC CEO Dana White talks to President-elect Donald Trump ringside during UFC 309 at Madison Square Garden. (Brad Penner/Imagn Images)

Trump said some people took to the idea after he floated it. "And then people said, you know, that’s actually a pretty good idea," he said.

The clarification came a day after Trump wrote on Truth Social that he had offered White the job. His post named UFC stars including Khabib Nurmagomedov, Islam Makhachev, Justin Gaethje, Daniel Cormier and Amanda Nunes as prospective assistants.

"Dana, please take the job!" Trump wrote.

White shared Trump’s post on his Instagram Story, along with a photo of himself behind the White House podium. He captioned the photo, "On to the next chapter."
